The benchmark's own test therefore checks that the record is **internally true** (every engine either carries measured numbers with its provenance, or says plainly that it could not be measured **here** and why), and not that a particular engine is absent. |
| D-110 | the extension ships the **engine and the Python path** as its defaults, so installing it is enough | locked | A setting's value lives in the user's settings; a **default** lives with the extension, and VS Code takes the default when the user has not chosen a value. So the two settings that name _this machine's_ tools - the OCR engine and the interpreter the Python engines run in - are the extension's to ship: the manifest declares them with the owner's values, and VS Code applies them until the teacher overrides them. The **token** is the one setting the extension must NOT ship: it is a **secret**, the `.vsix` is a **published** artifact (it lives in a public repository), and the token's whole purpose is to be **per-session** (D-50) - so it stays the teacher's, set once in the settings UI. The rule this records: **a setting that names a tool ships with the extension; a setting that holds a secret never does.** |
| D-111 | the app's token lives for the **tab's session**, so a reload does not ask for it again | locked | The app holds the token in a closure and nowhere else (D-50): never in `localStorage`, never in a URL, never in the document. The cost of that strictness is that **every page reload** asks the teacher to paste the token again - and the teacher reloads the page often (the extension changes, the dev server restarts). The browser already has a memory that is exactly right for this: **`sessionStorage`**, which belongs to the **tab**, survives a reload, and is dropped when the tab closes. It is still not `localStorage` (which would outlive the tab and the session), still not a URL, still not the document - so the secret's blast radius is unchanged, and the paste becomes **once per tab** instead of once per reload. The token is still never written to the document, never put in a URL, and still dies with the tab. |
| D-112 | the capture **re-resolves the editor's window** whenever it stops producing a picture, and the extension **restarts** a helper that has died | locked | The helper resolves the window once and then loops with that handle, so a window that is hidden, minimised or recreated (a Reload Window) leaves it holding a dead handle: `PrintWindow` returns black, or the helper exits with `NO_WINDOW` and the panel says _"The live view has not started yet"_. That is a **fact about a moment**, and it was being answered with a **permanent** failure. So the helper keeps the _rule_ for finding the window (the process, the title) and re-resolves the _handle_ whenever a capture comes back empty, and it does **not** exit when there is no window - it waits and says so. The extension restarts a helper that has exited, and re-arms the capture when a client connects, so a reload recovers **by itself** instead of needing a refocus. The rule: **a capture that cannot find its subject must keep looking, and the subject is found by rule, not remembered by handle.** |
| D-113 | the editor's window is found **by rule**, even when it is not the foreground window | locked | `Get-Process`'s `MainWindowHandle` is **0** for a hidden or minimised window, so the lookup that finds the editor while it is in front **loses it** the moment the browser takes the focus - which is the normal state while the teacher is looking at the whiteboard. So the window is found by **enumerating the editor's own top-level windows and matching the title** (the same title rule the project already uses elsewhere), and a window that exists but is not visible is a **different, stated** fact from a window that does not exist. The panel then says _"VS Code's window is not visible - click on it"_, not a black rectangle and not "not started yet". The rule: **"not in front" and "not there" are different facts, and neither of them is "broken".** |
| D-114 | every part of the chrome is sized by the **one** source of the scale, including the choice strip and the wireframe | locked | Wave 5k made the chrome's **sizes** screen-sized and put the one conversion in `scene/panelChrome.ts`. But the sizes only reach the DOM through the **inline styles**, and the parts added later - the screenshot's choice strip, the wireframe - were written without asking the chrome first: the strip's buttons got a height and a font size and **no width**, and the strip's `top` came from the caller. So a control that exists in the DOM is not a control the teacher can see or hit. So the rule is now stated for the whole chrome, not for the header: **nothing the chrome draws may size itself**; every size comes from `panelChrome.ts`, and a control that cannot be sized by the chrome must not be drawn. The strip's three buttons are laid out in a row with a real width each, and the strip hangs **on the panel** - its position is computed from the panel's own rect, never guessed. The test is a **measurement**, not an assertion about a style: a control that is **0 px wide** is a control that does not exist (the 0 px screenshot button of wave 5p was exactly this). |
| D-115 | the screenshot button belongs to the **panel**, not to the selection, and **every** panel kind has one | superseded by D-135 | The camera is a property of a panel (it captures that panel), so it must be reachable **whenever the panel is on screen** - not only after the teacher selects it. And a **video** panel can be captured (the owner's item 5.4: the video has no camera at all) - so the camera is on the video's chrome as well as the code's. The rule: **a control that acts on a panel lives on the panel**, visible and hit-testable without any prior selection. |
| D-116 | taking a screenshot **brings the screenshot forward**: the canvas darkens, the preview is the thing in focus, and the three choices belong to it | locked | The owner asked for the visual effect of a **z-axis**: "after clicking this button the preview of the screenshot should appear as if it has moved up in the z axis and the canvas/editor has moved back in the z - axis (visual effect only darkens background, leaving the screenshot to become the thing in focus with those 3 options i mentioned, also add a cropping tool that is turned on by default so we can crop the screenshot and revert the crop back one step aswell)". So the capture does not merely produce a picture: it opens a **focus state** - the canvas behind is dimmed, the captured picture is presented in front, the three choices act on **that** picture, and the picture can be **cropped** before it is placed (the crop is on by default, and one step takes it back). The crop is part of the picture's **own** state - it belongs to the screenshot, not to the canvas - and "revert one step" means the crop can be undone exactly once, to the uncropped capture. **AMENDED (wave 9a, D-339):** the focus state is the crop window: an opaque window of its own, with the choices in a bar on top; the dimming of the canvas and of the panel, and the strip on the panel, are gone. |
| D-117 | the zoom may go **below** the page's fit zoom: the page is the bound for **panning**, not for **zooming out** | locked | The camera is clamped to the page's rect, and the clamp also pins the **zoom** to the value at which the page exactly fits the viewport - so the − button is a no-op at that scale (the owner measured 56 %, 36 % and 73 % in the three window sizes) and the chrome can never be inspected at a small zoom. The page is the bound for **where the teacher may scroll to** (that is the paste-overflow rule, D-31), and it is not a bound on how far **out** the view may zoom: below the fit zoom the page is simply smaller on the screen, with more of it visible. So the zoom's **floor** becomes a fixed small value instead of the page-fit value, and the floor is one number in one place. |
| D-118 | the OCR **engine is chosen in the panel**, and the fastest engine is the default | locked | The engine was a **VS Code setting**, so choosing it meant leaving the whiteboard - and the owner had already asked "i dont see a easy to use ocr option to use it". So the panel's "Read the text" gains a **picker** beside it with the three engines, each carrying its **measured speed** as a colour (indra green, tesseract yellow, paddle red - the traffic lights the owner asked for), and the **default becomes the fastest engine** (indra), because a default that is the slowest of the three is a default nobody wants. The VS Code setting stays as the **fallback** for a machine with no picker. |
| D-119 | the mute button shows the **effective** state, and the volume at zero **is** muted | locked | The button showed `video.muted`, so a slider at 0 left it saying "unmuted" while the video was silent - the owner's exact report ("sliding the volume slider completely down does not show the mute icon"). The video's **effective** state is "muted" when it is muted **or** its volume is 0, and that is what the button shows and what clicking it acts on: clicking a slider-at-zero button unmutes **and** restores a usable volume. The rule: **the control shows the state the user experiences, not the field it happens to be bound to.** |
| D-120 | the chrome's controls that do nothing are **either wired or removed** | locked | The panel carried a **lock** icon that did nothing, and a right-hand corner that could not be used to resize the panel. A control that does not do anything is worse than no control: the teacher spends a click on it and learns nothing. So each of them is either made to work - the lock toggles the panel's lock (which the panel already has) - or **removed**, and the rule is: **a control must have an effect or must not be drawn.** The resize corner, if it is kept, resizes the panel and is a real hit target at the chrome's size. |
| D-121 | the canvas's own controls are laid out in the space the **sidebar leaves**, not under it | locked | The sidebar is **overlaid** on the editor container and the canvas is sized to the **whole** container, so the canvas's right-hand controls - the top toolbar's right end, the page bar's right end - land **underneath** the sidebar and can only be reached by closing the sidebar or scrolling to the far edge (the owner's exact report). So the canvas's layout is given the sidebar's width as a **reserved** space: the controls sit beside the sidebar, never beneath it, and the reservation is the sidebar's actual width, so it tracks the sidebar being opened and closed. |
| D-122 | the bridge says **which** editor it is, and the workspace's name is never blank | locked | The panel said _"Connected to Visual Studio Code ()"_ - the workspace's name was empty, so the teacher could not tell **which** folder (or which of two editors) the panel was showing. So the bridge's hello carries the workspace's name **when there is one** and says plainly that there is **no folder open** when there is not - never an empty pair of brackets. The rule: **an empty field is a bug; say what is missing.** |
| D-123 | the code panel's editor view is a **real editor**: the teacher's own text is a **draft** on the panel, and the mirrored text is only what a draft starts from | locked | The editor view is a **mirror**, so it can only ever show what another program wrote, and with no bridge there is nothing to show - a black box. The owner wants the panel to be usable by itself: write code in it, run it, read the output. So the panel's payload gains a **draft**: the teacher's own text for that panel. The mirror becomes the draft's **starting point** - when the teacher opens the editor on a panel that has never been edited, the mirrored text is there to edit; once they type, the draft is theirs and the mirror no longer overwrites it. The editor is **editable** in both cases, and the panel says which it is showing. The rule: **a panel that shows text must let the teacher change it, and the teacher's own text must never be overwritten by a mirror.** |
| D-124 | **Run** executes the draft in the workspace, with a timeout and a bounded output, and a missing runtime is an **explicit** state | locked | Running needs something to run the code, and the thing that can is the extension: it already runs programs in the teacher's workspace (`bridge:run`). So the panel's Run control sends the **draft** and the **language** to the extension, which writes it to a temporary file and runs it with that language's runtime **in the workspace**, and returns the output. The run has a **timeout** and a **bounded output** - a program that never ends, or one that prints a million lines, must not wedge the panel - and both facts are reported ("timed out after 10 s", "output truncated at 20,000 characters"). The runtimes are the ones **actually present on the machine**: the extension detects them (node, python, and the others the registry knows) and offers only those, and a language whose runtime is missing says so with what to install (D-91). The rule: **the panel never claims a run happened that did not, and a run that cannot happen says why.** |
| D-125 | the playback failure message is the browser's verdict about the **file**, never a verdict about the **container** | locked | The panel used to say "this browser cannot play a matroska video" - a claim that was **not measured** when it was written, and that is **false** for the common case: Chrome demuxes Matroska (measured: a blob URL and a data URL both play an H.264 `.mkv`, and `canPlayType("video/x-matroska")` answers `"maybe"`). The browser's `error` event says that **this file** could not be decoded, and the usual cause is a **codec** the build does not decode (a 10-bit profile, AV1, or a broken file), not the container. So the message says exactly that: the browser could not play **this file**, the usual cause is a codec, and the remedy that always works is transcoding to H.264/AAC in MP4. The `ffmpeg` command stays - it is the one command that always produces a file every browser plays - but it is a **remedy**, not a verdict on the container. The rule this records: **the browser's error is about the bytes, and a message that names a format as the cause must have measured that format first.** |
| D-126 | the panel has a **fallback** for the bytes, and it says whether the fallback worked | locked | The owner asked for both: a fallback, and the panel **mentioning** when the fallback also cannot play the file. The fallback is the **other way of reaching the same bytes**, and it is deliberately cheap: a media-backed panel asks the host for a **fresh** object URL (the one it had may be stale or revoked - the library lends the URL and revokes it when the panel goes away), and a file-backed panel re-reads the file store's `dataURL` and reaches the same bytes through an **object URL** (the browser decodes the base64 itself, so no base64 string is ever built). The fallback **never rebuilds a base64 string of a big file** - that is the 827 MiB of memory wave 5l removed - so it stays cheap even for a 620 MB video. The panel tries the primary source, then the fallback **once**, and then says **which** it tried and that both failed. A panel that says "this file could not be played" without saying whether the fallback was tried is a panel that hides half of what it knows (D-75, D-93). |
| D-127 | an error from a source that has already been **replaced** is not a failure of the panel | locked | The library lends its URL and revokes it when the panel goes away - and the revoke happens when the URL **changes** (`PanelOverlay.tsx`: the cleanup of the effect that depends on the URL). So the moment the fallback replaces the first URL, the first URL is revoked while the `<video>` element may still be pointing at it, and the element fires an `error` for a source that is already gone. That error is **not** a fact about the file, and reporting it would send the teacher chasing a format that plays perfectly well. So the error is judged against the URL that is **current**: an error from a URL the panel has already replaced is ignored, and only an error from the current source counts. The rule is the same one the whole project follows - **a fact about something that is no longer there is not a fact about the file** (D-75, D-93). |
| D-128 | a YouTube video is an **iframe of YouTube's own player**, and the panel never parses YouTube's pages | locked | The panel is a DOM overlay, so it can host an `<iframe>` exactly as it hosts the live view's `<img>`. An iframe is the **supported** way to play a YouTube video in a web page, which means the video half of this feature has **no** YouTube-specific code that can rot: there is no endpoint to call, no markup to parse, no signature to compute. The panel holds a **video id**, not a URL - the id is the only thing YouTube's own player needs - and the embed URL is built from it. The video therefore keeps working when YouTube changes anything, because nothing of ours talks to their pages. |
| D-129 | the captions have **three tiers**, and the panel says which tier answered | locked | The captions are the fragile half, so they are **best effort with an honest report**. Tier one: the **official Data API v3** with the teacher's own key (`excalidraw-bridge.youtubeApiKey`) - a versioned API, and for the teacher's **own** channel the captions are authorised by the owner, so this tier really works. Tier two: the **public `timedtext` endpoint**, best effort - and **empty in practice**: measured on 2026-10-24, eight probes of four videos gave eight empty bodies, so tier two is not a path that works today and the **key** is the remedy. Tier three: **`captions:none`**, with the reason - and the reason names the thing that was tried first (the Data API, when there is a key) and the way to get it, never the wrong thing. The tier that answered travels with the answer, so the panel can say _"these captions came from the public endpoint, which may be incomplete"_ or _"no captions: the API key is not set"_ - and the teacher is never left guessing why a video has no text. The rule: **a feature that depends on someone else's page must degrade in tiers, and must say which tier it is on.** |
| D-130 | an iframe panel takes the **focus** only while the teacher is using it, and gives it back | locked | Every other panel's content is an `<img>` or a `<video>`: they take the focus, and the canvas gets it back. An **iframe** is different - clicking it moves the focus into YouTube's document, and the whiteboard's keyboard (Ctrl+V, the arrow keys, Delete) stops reaching the canvas while it does. So the iframe panel follows the **same rule the sidebar already follows** (D-73): the panel takes the focus **while the teacher is inside it** and gives it back when the pointer leaves - the canvas takes the focus back, and the iframe's focus is blurred. The teacher's typing goes to the video while they are in it, and to the whiteboard the moment they are not. That is the whole rule: **a foreign document may borrow the focus, never keep it.** |
| D-131 | the YouTube video keeps its **whole** panel, and the caption note **floats over** it | locked | The panel is the **video**: it is 480x270, which is 16:9, and that is the aspect YouTube's own player renders at. The caption note is an **annotation** about the video, not a second thing beside it, so it **overlays** the video's top edge instead of taking a row of the panel: the iframe fills the panel (`position: absolute; inset: 0`) and the note is `position: absolute` over it. The rule: **an annotation never takes the picture's space** - a teacher who plays the video in a classroom must see the whole 16:9 picture, and the note is told to the side of it, not at the cost of it. |
| D-132 | the caption note is **one line**, and the whole reason is its **tooltip** | locked | The reason is a **sentence** - "no captions: the YouTube API key is not set and the public timedtext endpoint gave nothing back. Set excalidraw-bridge.youtubeApiKey to read them" - and at the panel's width it is **five lines**. Five lines of overlay would cover a third of the video, so the note is **one line with an ellipsis**, and the **whole** reason is the note's `title`: the tooltip says it all, the line says what fits. The rule: **a long reason is shown in the tooltip, and the line carries the short truth** - and the short truth here is the beginning of the reason, which is the part that matters ("no captions: the YouTube API key is not set..."). |
| D-133 | a panel never shows a **stale** connection: the connection line is replaced by the **truth** | locked | The connection line is a **status**, and a status is the truth **now** - but the status rides in the panel's payload, and the payload is **saved with the document**. So a document saved while the bridge was connected comes back with a line that says _"Connected to Visual Studio Code (my-todo-app, bridge 0.1.0)."_ on a machine where nothing is connected, and the teacher waits for a live window that is never coming. The rule: **the app writes its truth into the panels when it starts and whenever the connection changes** - so a panel that is not connected says **"not connected"**, whatever the document remembers. The saved line is history; the panel's line is the truth. |
| D-134 | the panel's OCR sentence names the engine the panel will **use**, not the extension's default | locked | The panel's engine picker (D-118) is the teacher's choice **for this panel**, and the panel sends that engine with every OCR request - so the engine that **runs** is the one the teacher picked. But the sentence under the row was built from the engine the **extension** reported in `ocr:state`, which is the extension's own setting (`excalidraw-bridge.ocrEngine`) - and that setting is only the **fallback** for a machine with no picker at all. So the panel could show `indra` in the picker and name `paddle` in the sentence, and the teacher would believe the slow engine runs (27,822 ms against 147 ms on this box). The rule: **the panel says what will happen** - the sentence names the engine the panel will use, which is the teacher's choice when there is one. |
| D-135 | a panel's **actions** live in the properties panel, and the picture never covers them | locked | A button drawn **inside** the panel overlay is a button on top of a picture, and on a YouTube panel the picture is an **iframe**: a foreign document that takes the click and gives it to YouTube. The button is visible and dead, and a teacher cannot tell a dead button from a broken one. The properties panel (`Panel / Kind / State / Live`) is ordinary DOM beside the canvas, so a button there always works. The rule: **the panel's actions belong to the panel's properties, not to the panel's picture** - the camera, the read and the crop are choices about a panel, and a choice about a panel belongs where the panel's other choices are. |
| D-136 | the bridge box is **not** drawn over the picture | locked | The bridge box (the token field and the Connect button) was drawn inside the panel overlay, so it covered the VS Code editor and the live window - the very things the teacher is looking at. The connection is a property of the **panel**, so the box belongs in the properties panel with the rest of them. The rule: **the chrome never covers the picture it describes.** |
| D-137 | the crop and the read are **one** action, and the engine is chosen by what the machine can actually do | locked | The owner asked for both in one breath: _"maybe we should combine the cropping and the ocr together, default uses tessaract, with vscode/vscodium bridge it can use indra and if nothing works they can use paddle"_. So the read takes the **cropped region** - one action, not two - and the engine is a **ladder**, not a setting the teacher has to know about: **indra** when the bridge is connected (147 ms on this box), **tesseract** when it is not (278 ms, and it needs nothing but the browser), and **paddle** only as the last resort (27,822 ms - and it is the one that needs the Python sidecar). The ladder walks itself and says which engine ran and why the one before it did not. The rule: **a teacher should never have to choose an engine; the machine chooses, and says what it chose.** Amended by D-294: paddle is not offered; the ladder is indra, then tesseract. |
| D-138 | a slow read is **visible**: the panel says it is reading, and the teacher is not left with a dead button | locked | PaddleOCR on this box takes **27,822 ms** - nearly half a minute - and a button that does nothing for half a minute looks exactly like a button that is broken (which is what the owner reported). So a read that is running shows a **loading state**: the control says it is reading, and it can be **cancelled**. The rule: **an action that takes time says so while it takes it.** |
| D-139 | the crop really **crops**: the handles change the picture that is placed | locked | The owner saw the crop handles and could not move them: _"cropping of images doesnot seem to work i can see the handles to crop the image but not do anything to it"_. A crop that is drawn but does not change the picture is a **promise the panel does not keep**, and the picture that lands on the canvas is the uncropped one. So the handles **are** the crop: dragging one changes the region, the region is what is read and what is placed, and one step takes the crop back (D-116). The rule: **a handle that cannot be moved must not be drawn; a handle that can be moved must change the result.** |
| D-140 | the placement flows are in the owner's **order**, and the picture hands the canvas back when it is placed | locked | The owner wrote the three flows out: _(crop image) -> insert image in this page -> (crop image) -> select where the screenshot goes to_, and the popup must **vanish** and return to the canvas; _(crop image) -> read text in this cropped region -> select where the extracted text goes to_; and _(crop image) -> insert image in another page -> select the page (through the page selecter ribbon) -> (crop image) -> select where the screenshot goes to_. So the **crop comes first**, the target is chosen **after** it, and the flow that writes **text** asks where the **text** goes - not where the picture would go. The wireframe ("the screenshot goes here") is a **guide on the canvas**, so it must not compete with the panels in the same z-plane (D-116): it is above them and the canvas is dimmed behind it. The rule: **the teacher chooses the target last, and a finished placement gives the canvas back.** **AMENDED (wave 9a, D-339):** the choices are in the crop window's bar, New page goes straight to the rectangle (no ribbon), and the rectangle follows the pointer and is placed by a click. |
| D-141 | the presenter view **works**, and it is the same canvas the teacher is teaching | locked | The owner reported, with the failure in a screenshot, that _"somehow the presenter view is dead"_. A presenter view that does not open, or opens onto a different canvas than the teacher's, is worse than none: the teacher's students see something else. So the presenter view opens, shows the **same** scene and the **same** page, and its own controls work. The rule: **the presenter view is the teacher's canvas, seen by someone else.** |
| D-142 | the live editor is a **real editor**: the text can be selected, copied as a code block, and read in the language the teacher chooses | locked | The owner: _"the live editor is weird i can see the text from the connection to vscode/vscodium scroll through that text, cannot select that text and paste it as a codeblock (same as when i select copy code or use the insert as code block in the markdown viewer), nor select the programming language of my choice"_. So the mirrored text is **read-only** and the teacher can do nothing with it: not select it, not copy it as a code block, not read it as a chosen language, and not zoom into it for a long file. The rule: **text the teacher can see is text the teacher can take.** |
| D-143 | the teacher chooses **what** is mirrored, and the choice can follow the cursor | locked | The owner: _"why is the vscode/vscodium what i want section from the vscode that i want to mirror to the canvas option not available? i.e; it should show me the full vscode window (same as when i take a screenshot - only when i click change what is mirrored button or something) and i crop the section i want mirrored (i should be able to edit it as many times as possible, and maybe it can even have a follow the cursor option that dynamically moves the mirrored section based on the position of the mouse that is being moved inside the panel)"_. So the panel mirrors the **whole** window, and there is no way to say _"this part"_. The rule: **what is mirrored is a choice, and a choice can be changed as often as the teacher changes their mind.** The follow-the-cursor option is a **mode**, not a one-off: the region follows the pointer inside the panel, and the teacher can turn it off. **AMENDED (wave 9a, D-334):** the picker is drawn into the full-screen panel when the panel is full screen, and the teacher does not leave it. **AMENDED (wave 9a, D-337):** the following area rests in the middle half of the window and moves by steps outside it. |
| D-144 | a page has a **frame**, a **number**, and a **size** | locked | The owner's answer, and the model for it is Microsoft Word's: _"pages visible frame can be toggled on/off with a page number etc (the same options one gets in the microsoft word, header/fotter -> page number thingy), ok include presets for width and height. (this should help the export page as now the format in which the text appears would be different based on the preset selected)"_. So a page may be drawn with a visible **frame**, may carry a **page number**, and has a **size** the teacher picks - from **presets** (A4, Letter, 16:9, ...) or by hand - and the preset changes the **export**, because the text in a 16:9 page is not the text in an A4 page. The rule: **the page's shape is a fact about the page, and the export must honour it.** |
| D-145 | the virtual display and the **input panel** are built | locked | The owner approved this: _"yes work on the virtual display+input panel"_. The idea: a **virtual display** - a screen the teacher can show on a projector without occupying the real desktop - and an **input panel** beside it, so the teacher's controls for the virtual screen are not on the virtual screen. The rule: **the screen the students see and the controls the teacher uses are different surfaces.** Wave 9a: the virtual display half of this row is gone (D-329) and the input panel stays; the screens are told apart by parking the editor's window (D-328). |
| D-146 | an **installed but detached** virtual display is reported, with the click that attaches it | superseded by D-329 | The teacher may have a virtual display driver **installed** and its monitor **detached** - VDD Control keeps a monitor detached until the teacher sets its mode to Extend, and until then the monitor is not part of the desktop's coordinate space, so a window cannot be placed on it. Today the helper skips an adapter with no current mode, so the tool says "there is no virtual display" and the teacher is left thinking they have to install something they already installed. The rule: **a driver that is installed but not attached is still a fact, and the fact names the click.** The extension reports the adapter as a virtual display that is **detached**, and the reason says exactly what to do. |
| D-147 | a window can be moved onto the virtual display **by rule**, and the move never takes the focus | locked | The teacher asked for the **VS Code window** to move to the virtual display, not only the presenter. The capture helper already finds a window **by rule** (D-112, D-113) and the virtual-display helper already places a window **without activating it** (SWP_NOACTIVATE). The rule: **the same rule finds the editor's window** - its title carries the workspace name - and the same placement moves it onto the virtual display **without taking the focus**, so the teacher keeps typing in their editor while their window is on the projector. Wave 9a: the same rule finds the editor's window to park it (D-328), and the move still never takes the focus. |
| D-148 | moving the teacher's **own** window is an **option the teacher chooses** | locked | The teacher's own decision: _"moving the editor's window is an OPTION the teacher chooses. It is NEVER the default."_ The default stays what it was - the presenter's window may be placed on the virtual display (D-145) - and the teacher's editor window is not touched: it stays exactly where they put it, on the display they are working on. The rule: **nothing moves unless the teacher pressed the button.** There is no automatic placement of the editor and no default that relocates their window. Wave 9a: parking is the same kind of option (D-328) - nothing is parked unless the teacher pressed the button. |
| D-149 | a window that was moved can be **brought back** | locked | The teacher's own decision: _"once a window is on the virtual display, the teacher can get it BACK. The feature is not a one-way door."_ The round trip is: un-maximise, move it back to the primary display, and - when it was maximised before it moved - maximise it again, so the window comes back the way the teacher had it. The rule: **the teacher's window is theirs, in both directions.** Wave 9a: a parked window comes back the same way (D-328). |
| D-150 | a move that **did not happen** is never reported as one | locked | The teacher measured it on this machine with their own window: `SetWindowPos` on a **maximised** window returns `true` and the window does not move - Windows puts it back to the work area of the display it was on. A tool that believes the return value tells the teacher their window is on the projector when it is not. The rule: **the move is measured, not believed.** A maximised window is un-maximised first (without taking the focus), the rect is read back with `GetWindowRect`, the **measured** rect is what is reported, and a window that did not land where it was asked to is said so plainly - the same way D-145 makes the tool say "no virtual display" rather than fail quietly. This holds for the **presenter's** window too: it is maximised in the normal case, so it has the same trap. Wave 9a: a park is measured the same way (D-328). |

## Acceptance — Waves 6b to 6i (the owner's review of 2026-10-24)

The owner's review of 2026-10-24 is recorded in full, with the diagnosis for each item, in `docs/tins-tasks/2026-10-24-owner-review-2.md`. The decisions are already written down: the pages get a frame, a number and **presets** (D-144), and the virtual display + input panel is **approved** (D-145).

Each wave below has **its own section**, and each wave adds **its own row** to its own section - so the waves can be built side by side and the SPEC merges cleanly.

### Wave 6b — one crop-and-read action, and the engine ladder

The owner asked for both in one breath: _"maybe we should combine the cropping and the ocr together, default uses tessaract, with vscode/vscodium bridge it can use indra and if nothing works they can use paddle put in a loading animation so they know when to expexct the text to be come visible again."_ So the read takes the **cropped** region - one action, not two - the engine is a **ladder** the machine walks by itself (D-137), and a slow read says so while it happens (D-138). The acceptance row for this wave is **AC-175**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-175 | the read takes the cropped region, the engine is a ladder the machine walks itself, and a slow read is visible and cancellable | `excalidraw-app/tests/ocrBridge.test.tsx` | The crop and the read are **one** action: the panel's read asks for the **cropped region** - the crop the teacher took for that panel - and not the whole picture, so the read is the one the teacher meant (D-137). The engine is a **ladder**, not a setting the teacher has to know: **indra** when the bridge is connected (147 ms), **tesseract** when it is not (278 ms); paddle, once the last resort (27,822 ms), is not offered any more (D-294) - and every rung that is skipped is skipped for a **named reason**, because a silent fall-back is what this project keeps refusing (D-75, D-93, D-125). The answer says **which engine ran**, **how long it took**, and **why the one before it did not**. And a read that takes time says so while it takes it: the control shows the **loading state** and it can be **cancelled**, and cancelling leaves the panel usable (D-138). Tests: `AC-175 the read takes the cropped region`, `AC-175 the engine ladder runs indra with the bridge, then tesseract, and no other`, `AC-175 the answer says which engine ran and why the one before it did not`, `AC-175 a slow read shows the loading state and can be cancelled`. |

### Wave 6c — the crop really crops, and the placement flows are in the owner's order

_"cropping of images doesnot seem to work i can see the handles to crop the image but not do anything to it"_, and the three placement flows written out in full: the crop first, the target last, and the popup must vanish and hand the canvas back (D-139, D-140). The acceptance rows for this wave are **AC-176** and **AC-178**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-176 | the crop **really crops** — a handle that can be moved changes the picture that is placed — and the wireframe stands **above** the panels, with the canvas dimmed behind it | `packages/excalidraw/tests/screenshotCrop.test.tsx` | The owner saw the handles and could not move them: _"i can see the handles to crop the image but not do anything to it"_ (D-139). The focus state was drawn **under** the interactive canvas and **under** the panels, so the handles were visible and dead; the focus state is now **above** both, the picture in front shows **only the cropped part**, and the crop is the **picture's own** state with one step of revert (D-116). The wireframe is a guide on the canvas: it is **above** the panels and the canvas is dimmed behind it (D-140). Tests: `AC-176 dragging a crop handle changes the crop`, `AC-176 the picture in front shows only the cropped part`, `AC-176 the crop is the screenshot's own state and reverts one step`, `AC-176 the wireframe stands above the panels with the canvas dimmed behind it`. **AMENDED (wave 9a, D-339):** there is no dimming of the canvas any more; the wireframe stands above the panels and the crop window above the whole app. Test: `AC-176 the wireframe stands above the panels, and the crop window above the whole app`. |
| AC-178 | the three placement flows run in the **owner's order**: the crop first, the target last, and a finished placement **hands the canvas back** | `excalidraw-app/tests/screenshotFlows.test.tsx` | The owner wrote the three flows out (D-140): the crop comes first, the target is chosen **after** it, the flow that writes **text** asks where the **text** goes, and the third flow goes through the **page selector ribbon**. The wireframe appears only once the teacher has chosen, the text is put where the teacher says and not where the picture would go, and a finished placement closes the popup and gives the canvas back. Tests: `AC-178 the wireframe waits for the choice`, `AC-178 the flow that reads the text asks where the text goes`, `AC-178 the third flow goes through the page selector ribbon`, `AC-178 a finished placement closes the popup and hands the canvas back`. |

### Wave 6d — the presenter view works

_"somehow the presenter view is dead this is what the failure is about"_ - the presenter view is the teacher's canvas, seen by someone else (D-141). The acceptance row for this wave is **AC-177**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-177 | the presenter view opens, shows the teacher's scene and page, and its own controls work | `excalidraw-app/tests/presenter.test.tsx` | The presenter is the teacher's canvas, seen by someone else (D-141), and a control that does nothing is worse than no control (D-120). Tests: `AC-177 the presenter view opens and shows the current scene`, `AC-177 the presenter view follows the page the teacher is on`, `AC-177 the presenter's own controls work`, `AC-177 the presenter's fullscreen control is offered only where the browser can do it`. |

### Wave 6e — the live editor is a real editor

_"the live editor is weird ... cannot select that text and paste it as a codeblock ... nor select the programming language of my choice, zoom in/ zoom out both the page and inside the editor"_ (D-142). The acceptance row for this wave is **AC-179**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-179 | the mirrored text can be **selected and pasted as a code block**, the **language** can be chosen, and the editor can be **zoomed inside** the panel | `packages/excalidraw/tests/codePanel.test.tsx` `excalidraw-app/tests/codePanel.test.tsx` | The live editor is a **real editor** (D-142): the text in the panel can be selected, copied, and pasted on the canvas as the **same code card** the markdown viewer's `Copy code` and `insert as code block` bring - the app's own armed paste, and not a second kind of element. The panel's own **editor bar** carries the three things the teacher asked for: the **language** the file is **read** as (the file in the editor is never changed by it), a **Copy as code block** control, and the editor's own **zoom** - which is the editor's and never the canvas's (D-117). The teacher's own **draft** is never touched by any of it (D-123): the copy takes the **selection** (or the whole text when nothing is selected), the mirror never overwrites the draft, and the language the teacher chooses is the panel's own reading of the file and not the draft's. Tests: `AC-179 the selected text is copied as a code block`, `AC-179 the panel reads the file as the language the teacher chose`, `AC-179 the editor zooms inside the panel and the canvas zoom stays where it was`, `AC-179 the panel's copy pastes on the canvas as the same code card`. **AMENDED (wave 9a, D-331):** the text that is selected and copied is the code editor's own. |

### Wave 6f — what is mirrored, and follow the cursor

_"why is the vscode/vscodium what i want section ... not available? ... i crop the section i want mirrored (i should be able to edit it as many times as possible, and maybe it can even have a follow the cursor option)"_ (D-143). The acceptance row for this wave is **AC-182**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-182 | the teacher chooses **what** is mirrored - the whole window by default, a region of their choosing, changed as often as they like - and the region can **follow the cursor** | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/bridge.test.ts` `packages/excalidraw/tests/codePanel.test.tsx` | What is mirrored is a **choice**, and a choice can be changed as often as the teacher changes their mind (D-143). With no region set the panel mirrors the **whole** editor window - the same picture a screenshot gives - and the teacher's own control, **change what is mirrored**, shows that whole window so they can see what they are choosing; the crop they draw is the region that is mirrored from then on, and they can draw another one whenever they want. The region is the **panel's own** setting, it is measured in **fractions of the whole window** so it is the same region whatever size the window is, and it travels to the editor over the bridge so the capture descriptor can say what is mirrored. **Follow the cursor** is a **mode**: it is **off by default**, the teacher turns it on, the mirrored region moves with the pointer inside the panel, and they can turn it off again. The editor's window is still found **by rule** (D-112, D-113): a region never changes how the window is found. Tests: `AC-182 the whole window is mirrored until the teacher chooses a region`, `AC-182 the region travels to the editor and comes back in the capture descriptor`, `AC-182 the region can be changed again and again`, `AC-182 the follow the cursor mode is off until the teacher turns it on`, `AC-182 the panel shows only the region the teacher chose`, `AC-182 the mirrored region follows the pointer while the follow mode is on`, `AC-182 the follow mode is off until the teacher turns it on`. **AMENDED (wave 9a, D-337):** the area rests while the pointer is in the middle half of the window, and the test moves the pointer near the edge to see the step. |

### Wave 6g — a page has a frame, a number and a size

The owner's decision: _"pages visible frame can be toggled on/off with a page number etc (the same options one gets in the microsoft word, header/fotter -> page number thingy), ok include presets for width and height"_ (D-144). The acceptance row for this wave is **AC-180**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-180 | a page has a **frame**, a **number** and a **size**, and the export honours the size | `packages/excalidraw/tests/pages.test.tsx` | The frame and the number are facts **about the page**, saved with the document (D-144): the frame is the page's own rectangle, the number is the page's own number in the page's footer, and both are toggled from the Page section of the properties panel. The size is chosen from a preset (A4 portrait and landscape, Letter portrait and landscape, 16:9, 4:3 and the project's own 1920x1080) or typed by hand; a preset fixes the page's **shape**, and because a page may only grow (D-1) the shape is scaled up to the minimum page size, so the exported image comes out in the preset's shape. Tests: `AC-180 the page frame can be toggled on and off`, `AC-180 the page number says the page's own number`, `AC-180 a size preset shapes the exported page`. |

### Wave 6h — the virtual display and the input panel

_"yes work on the virtual display+input panel"_ - the screen the students see and the controls the teacher uses are different surfaces (D-145). The acceptance row for this wave is **AC-181**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-181 | the **input panel** drives the presenter from the teacher's own screen | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/bridge.test.ts` | The screen the students see and the controls the teacher uses are different surfaces (D-145): the input panel is served on 127.0.0.1 behind a capability and never with the token, and its controls drive the presenter over the bridge. The part of this row that placed the presenter's window on a virtual display is gone with the driver (D-329). ### Wave 6i — the installed but detached virtual display, and the editor's window

The owner installed the **Virtual Display Driver** (release 25.7.23, "Generic Monitor (VDD by MTT)") on this machine, and asked for the VS Code window to move to it. The driver is installed and its monitor is **detached** - VDD Control keeps a monitor detached until the teacher sets its mode to **Extend** - so the extension had nothing to report and said "no virtual display" (D-146). The teacher then set it to Extend, and measured a second truth on their own window: `SetWindowPos` on a **maximised** window returns `true` and the window does not move, so a move that did not happen must never be reported as one (D-150), and a window that was moved must be able to come **back** (D-149). The rule: **an installed but detached virtual display is a fact, and the fact names the click** (D-146); the **editor's own window** moves only when the teacher asks for it (D-148), **by rule** and without taking the focus (D-147); and the move is **measured**, not believed (D-150). The acceptance row for this wave is **AC-183**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-183 | the editor's window is moved **by rule** and only when the teacher asks, and a window that was moved can be **brought back** | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/windowPark.test.ts` | The editor's own window is found **by rule** - the workspace name in the window's title, the same rule the capture uses (D-112, D-113) - and it is moved without taking the focus (SWP_NOACTIVATE), so the teacher keeps typing while their window is out of sight (D-147). It moves **only when the teacher asks** (D-148), and it comes **back** in the size it had, maximised as it was if it was maximised (D-149). The move is **measured**: a maximised window is un-maximised first without taking the focus, the rect is read back, and a window that did not land where it was asked to is said so plainly (D-150). The place it goes to is a parking place off every display (D-328) and not a virtual display (D-329). Tests: `AC-183 the editor's window is moved without taking the focus`, `AC-183 a window that was moved can be brought back, and a move that did not happen is not reported as one`. |

### Wave 6j — what the live clips found

The feature clips for the public release were recorded by **driving the real app with a real editor behind it**. Doing that surfaced five defects the tests did not, and two of them mean a **shipped install does not work at all**: the `.vsix` does not carry the virtual-display helper, so every install has a **dead virtual display**; and the snapshot endpoint sends no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, so a **stock** browser refuses the app's `fetch()` of the editor's picture - which breaks "take a screenshot of the editor's window" and "crop and read" from the editor. The other three are smaller but real: the mirror picker's choice buttons are painted under the app's bottom page bar, the YouTube caption note is painted under the composited iframe and its background is a theme variable that does not exist, and the input panel's status line writes to `window.status` and so shows nothing. The rule: **the extension ships every helper it runs, and the bridge answers the app's own origin** (D-151).

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-151 | the extension ships **every helper it runs**, and the bridge answers the app's **own** origin | locked | A helper that is not in the `.vsix` is a feature that is **dead in every install**: the extension resolves its helpers (`media/window.ps1`, `media/input.ps1`, `media/capture.ps1`) inside its own installed folder, and the folder has no such file - so the panel says "no virtual display was found on this machine ... and no display was found at all" on a machine whose Virtual Display Driver is attached as `DISPLAY7`. And the extension's HTTP server must answer the **same loopback origins** its websocket answers: the app is on `http://localhost:3001` and the server is on `http://127.0.0.1:47511`, so the picture the app is **entitled** to fetch was refused by the browser as a cross-origin read - and the only way to record the crop-and-read clip was Chromium's `--disable-web-security` flag, which is how the defect was found. The rule: the **capability stays the authorisation** (D-86) and the server still binds the **loopback interface only**, but a request that carries a live capability and comes from a **loopback** origin is answered with its own origin echoed back, and a request from a **foreign** origin is answered with **no** such header. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-184 | the built `.vsix` **contains** the helpers the extension runs (`media/window.ps1`, `media/input.ps1`, `media/capture.ps1`) and not the virtual display's - and the test reads the `.vsix`'s **entry list**, not its bytes | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/vsix.test.ts` | The `.vsix` is a zip, and the file it carries is read from the zip's **central directory** - the same CRC-checking reader the other tests use. A byte search is not a proof here: the helper's **name** appears inside `extension.js` (the extension resolves the helper by that relative path), so a byte search gives a **false positive**. The entry list is what the installer unpacks, so the entry list is what is checked. Tests: `AC-184 the .vsix ships the window helper and the input helper`, `AC-184 the .vsix does not ship the virtual-display helper`. |
| AC-185 | the snapshot's response carries an `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` for a **loopback** origin, and **omits** it for a **foreign** one | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/stream.test.ts` | The snapshot is the picture the app fetches with a plain `GET` (`fetch()`), so the browser applies the cross-origin rules to it - while the live view is an `<img>`, which is fetched in "no-cors" mode and is not affected. The **same** rule that decides the websocket's origin decides this header: `isAllowedOrigin(origin, extra)`, so a loopback origin (`http://localhost:3001`) gets **its own origin echoed back** and a foreign one (`https://evil.example.com`) gets **no** header at all. The answer also says that the picture's **own size** may be read (`Access-Control-Expose-Headers`): a header that is not CORS-safelisted is hidden from the page's JavaScript, so without it the app would read a size of zero for a picture it can see (D-102). The capability is still the authorisation (D-86): a request without a live capability is still a 404, for every origin. Tests: `AC-185 the snapshot answers a loopback origin with its own origin echoed back`, `AC-185 a foreign origin is answered with no Access-Control-Allow-Origin`. |

### Wave 7k — the setup checks, and installs what is missing

The people this project is for will **not read a README**: a teacher or a student with a laptop, VS Code or VSCodium, and a class to teach. Today they would have to know, on their own, that the project needs Node, that Yarn comes from Corepack, that the extension is installed from a `.vsix`, and that the app's dependencies have to be installed before the app will start. So the project ships a **setup**: one command that **checks what is missing and installs it**. The rule: **the setup reports what it finds, asks before it touches the machine, and never installs software silently** (D-152).

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-152 | the project ships a **setup** that checks for everything it needs and **installs what is missing** | locked | The person who runs the setup is a **teacher or a student**, not a developer, so the setup **reports what it finds** - every thing it checks, in plain words, with the version it found and where it found it - **asks before it installs anything on the machine**, and ends with a **summary** the reader can act on. It never installs software silently: the default is a **question**, `-Yes` accepts the answers, and `-DryRun` reports and installs **nothing** at all. It needs **no dependency of its own** - PowerShell on Windows, bash on macOS and Linux, and nothing else - so it runs from a fresh clone, and **checking never needs the network** (only installing does). It is **safe to run twice**: every check is idempotent, and a second run says _already there_ rather than doing it again. What it checks: Node.js on the PATH **and 20 or newer**, Yarn on the PATH or Corepack to provide it, VS Code (the `code` CLI or the standard install path), VSCodium (the `codium` CLI or the standard install path), the extension's `.vsix` (**built** when it is not there), the app's dependencies (`node_modules` at the repository root), and the **language runtimes** the panel's **Run** can execute (`node`, `python`, `java`). What it installs: Corepack for Yarn, the `.vsix` into whichever editor it found, the app's dependencies, and a missing runtime through **winget** when winget is there - each of them **after asking**. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-186 | the setup **reports** every thing it checks, **asks** before installing, installs **nothing** in `-DryRun`, and the report ends with a summary - and it needs **no dependency of its own** | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/setup.test.ts` | The **checking logic** is a module the test can call - `installer/lib/check.mjs` - and it is a **pure function of what was found**: it takes the versions and the paths the machine has and returns the **report** (what is present, what is missing, what would be installed) and the **plan** (what to ask, what to install, and what a dry run installs - nothing). It **never installs anything**, so the test can feed it a made-up machine and assert on the report: the test needs neither Windows nor a network. The two scripts - `installer/setup.ps1` and `installer/setup.sh` - are the halves that **find** the facts, **ask** and **install**, and the test pins them to the module: every check the module knows about is a check both scripts make, and no script can install anything before its dry-run guard. Tests: `AC-186 the report names every thing the setup checks`, `AC-186 a missing thing is reported as missing with what would be installed`, `AC-186 the setup asks before it installs anything`, `AC-186 a dry run installs nothing`, `AC-186 the report ends with a summary`, `AC-186 the setup needs no dependency of its own`, `AC-186 both scripts check the same things the module checks`, `AC-186 the Windows script's dry run installs nothing`. |

### Wave 7l — the setup as one file, the container, and the guide

The setup of wave 7k **reports** what is missing and installs it - but only where a **package manager** exists, and only for the things that are already on the machine. Recording the end-to-end test in a **Windows Sandbox** - a bare Windows with nothing on it, not even winget - showed what that costs: on such a machine the setup could only _report_, and it had no way to install the **editors** at all. The promise is that the setup installs what is missing, so it must install **everywhere**, not only where a package manager happens to be. And it must be **one file** a teacher can double-click, and the app must also be available as a **container** for a machine on which nothing can be installed at all.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-153 | the setup ships **as one file**, it installs **without a package manager**, and the app also ships as a **container** | locked | Three things follow from the promise. **One file**: the setup is also an **executable** (setup.exe), so it can be double-clicked; it is compiled by the **C# compiler that ships with Windows**, so the project gains **no dependency**, and it carries the installer scripts **inside** it. **Without a package manager**: where winget or brew is there it is used, and where neither is there the setup **downloads the official installer itself** - Node.js from nodejs.org, VS Code and VSCodium from their own sites, each for **the machine's own architecture** (x64 or rm64) - so a bare machine is set up as well as an ordinary one. The **editors themselves** are now something the setup installs, because a teacher with neither VS Code nor VSCodium cannot use the bridge. **A container**: the app is published as an image, so a machine on which nothing may be installed can still serve it; the extension stays on the **host**, because it lives inside the editor, and the container reaches the host's bridge over host.docker.internal. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-187 | the setup **is** one executable, it installs on a machine with **no package manager**, and the container's **build context carries the installer** and has the **heap** the build needs | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/setup.test.ts` | The test reads the **files**: the launcher's source and the build script beside it, the `.dockerignore` and the `Dockerfile`. It asserts that the setup is built by the compiler that ships with Windows - so the project gains no dependency - that the launcher carries the installer scripts with it, that the build context **includes** `installer/` (without it the test's own import cannot resolve inside the build), and that the build is given the **heap** it needs (the build inside a container dies at Node's default 2 GB while the same build on a developer's machine succeeds). The **rules** are pinned too: on a machine with no package manager the setup still installs Node and the editors, because it can download the official installers itself. Tests: `AC-187 the setup is one executable built by the compiler that ships with Windows`, `AC-187 the launcher carries the installer with it`, `AC-187 the container's build context carries the installer and the heap the build needs`, `AC-187 a machine with no package manager is still set up`. |

### Wave 7m — the offline setup: one file, no internet, nothing to install

The setup of wave 7l **downloads** what it installs. A classroom machine may have no internet at all: a locked-down lab, a school network, a laptop on a train. So the project must ship a setup that **carries everything it needs with it** and installs it **offline** - and must also be able to run the app with **nothing installed at all**.

The inventory that decides the payload was taken from the code, not guessed. **The app's npm dependencies are NOT needed at run time**: the app is a **static bundle** served as HTML and assets, so the payload does not carry the npm registry's tarballs and the offline setup never runs `yarn install`. **Python is NOT needed either**: the default OCR engine, _tesseract_, is **WebAssembly inside the extension** and runs in the extension's own process; Python is only for the optional `indra` and `paddle` engines. What the payload therefore carries is the **prebuilt app**, the **`.vsix`**, and the **installers** for Node, VS Code, VSCodium, Python, Java and the virtual display driver.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-154 | the project ships a setup that **needs no internet** and needs **nothing installed** to run the app | locked | Three things follow from the promise. **It carries the payload**: the payload is carried **in pieces** - resources of the executable, `payload.NNNN`, a few megabytes each, with an index (`payload.index.json`) saying which pieces make up which file - and the pieces are written **straight** into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\ExcalidrawClassroom\payload` on the first run, one piece at a time, with each file's **SHA-256 checked as it is written**. A payload that does not match is **refused**, not installed. (Pieces, and not one big resource, because the payload is about 780 MB: a single resource that size has to be held in memory by the runtime and the disk would have to hold it twice - once as the resource, once unpacked - which a small machine cannot do. In pieces, the disk holds the payload once and the memory holds one piece.) **It never reaches the internet**: the internet is needed only when the payload is **built**, by `installer/offline/build-payload.mjs`, which is re-runnable (a file that is already there is skipped) and writes the manifest with each file's hash and version. A file that is missing from the payload is **reported**, never downloaded. The installs are the ones the brief names - Node through `msiexec`, the editors through their own installers, Python through its own, Java from a Temurin zip unpacked into the machine's PATH, the virtual display driver through **its own** installer, and the extension through the editor's `--install-extension` - each **asked for one at a time**, with `-Yes` to accept and `-DryRun` to report and install **nothing**; the parts that need it (the driver, the machine-wide PATH) **elevate the program** with the `runas` verb. The driver needs **administrator rights** and a **reboot**, and the program says both plainly and does not claim the display works before the reboot. **It runs the app with nothing installed**: a built-in `HttpListener` serves the prebuilt app out of the payload and the browser is opened on it - no Node, no Python, no editor, just the whiteboard. And because the app's bridge URL is **baked in at build time** (`VITE_APP_BRIDGE_URL`), the program **prints the URL the app was built with** and says so plainly when it does not match the bridge the user will actually run - it does not pretend. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-188 | the offline setup **carries its payload**, **checks the payload's hashes**, **installs nothing in `-DryRun`**, **runs the app with nothing installed**, and the payload builder **needs no internet at the time it runs** - only at the time it is BUILT | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/offline.test.ts` | The test reads the **files** - the C# source, the payload builder and the manifest - and asserts on them. It **downloads nothing** and does **not run the payload**: the test proves the SHAPE, and the real run - a Windows Sandbox with **no networking** - proves the thing. The shape it proves is: the C# is **C# 5** (the compiler that ships with Windows understands nothing newer), it carries the payload **in pieces** and writes them out one at a time, it **verifies the SHA-256** of each file as it writes it and **refuses** a payload that does not match, it has **no network code at all**, every install is behind the **`-DryRun` guard**, it **serves the app** with an `HttpListener`, it **prints the bridge URL**, and it **elevates** with the `runas` verb. The payload builder is pinned too: the **URLs** it uses, the **architecture** it takes, that it is **re-runnable**, that it writes the **hash and the version** of each file, and that it never runs `yarn install` - the payload carries no npm tarballs, because the app is a static bundle. The manifest's hash is checked against **node's own SHA-256** of the file, so a manifest that hashes the wrong thing fails. Tests: `AC-188 the offline setup carries its payload with it`, `AC-188 the offline setup checks the payload's hashes and refuses a payload that does not match`, `AC-188 a dry run installs nothing at all`, `AC-188 the offline setup runs the app with nothing installed`, `AC-188 the offline setup never reaches the internet`, `AC-188 the payload builder downloads what the setup needs and is re-runnable`, `AC-188 the manifest carries the hash and the version of every file`, `AC-188 the offline setup is C# 5 and needs no dependency of its own`. |

### Wave 7n — how to confirm it works

The setup of wave 7m installs the app and serves it, but a teacher who installs it on **another** machine is left with two questions the project cannot answer yet. **Is the install sound?** The setup's own report is a by-product of the install: it is gone once the install is over, and it is not a thing a teacher can run afterwards. **Is the behaviour the expected behaviour?** The project has no written description of what each feature should look like, so a deviation stays invisible until a lesson goes wrong - and then nobody can say what the right thing was. Both answers must be readable by a **teacher**, not by a developer, and both live in the **public** repository, so they carry no trace of how the project is built.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-155 | the project ships **a check the teacher runs after the install** and **a checklist of what each feature should look like** | locked | Two documents, both written for a **reader**. **The check** is `installer/verify-install.ps1`: one command, no dependency of its own, and it **only reads** - it installs nothing, changes nothing and removes nothing. It prints a table of one row per check - the check, the expected value, the actual value, and the verdict - and the verdict is one of **PASS** (it is there and it is right), **MISSING** (it is not installed, which is often fine) or **FAIL** (it is there but it is **wrong**, which is the one that needs attention). A missing thing is a row in the table and never an exception, so the script survives a machine where half of it is absent, and it exits 1 only when something FAILED. It ends with a **paste-able report** - the machine's facts, the table and the counts - because that block is how a deviation gets reported. **The checklist** is `docs/VERIFY.md`: for each feature, what to do, what you should see, what a deviation looks like, and the clip that shows it working. The expected values are read from the **code**, never from memory: the Node floor from `installer/lib/check.mjs`, the editor floor from the extension's own `engines.vscode`, the port from the bridge's own `DEFAULT_BRIDGE_PORT`, the extension's name from its own `package.json`, and the hashes from the payload's own `manifest.json`. **The report page** is `docs/REPORT.md`: what you did, what you expected, what you actually saw, which feature, the check report and the machine - and it never asks for the session token, because the token is a secret. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-189 | the install check **reports every thing it checks**, **never changes the machine**, says **MISSING** instead of failing when a thing is absent, and the checklist gives **what to do**, **what you should see** and **what a deviation looks like** for every feature the project has | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/verify.test.ts` | The test reads the **files** - the script, the checklist and the report page - and asserts on them; it does not run the script, so it needs neither Windows nor the machine's editor. It pins the **rules**: the script installs nothing and changes nothing, every check is guarded so that a missing thing is a row and not an exception, the three verdicts are the ones the legend explains, the script exits 1 only when a check FAILED, the expected values are read from the code rather than written into the script, the report at the end is paste-able, and the checklist gives every feature all four parts and points at a clip. Tests: `AC-189 the check script only reads the machine`, `AC-189 a missing thing is a row and never an exception`, `AC-189 the script ends with a paste-able report and exits 1 only on a failure`, `AC-189 the expected values are read from the code`, `AC-189 the checklist gives every feature its four parts and its clip`, `AC-189 the report page never asks for the session token`. |

### Wave 7o - the offline proof starts from a fresh sandbox

The proof that the offline setup works on a machine with no internet (wave 7m) was **reached**, but not **repeatably**, and the record did not say the whole truth. The `.wsb` asks for no networking, and on the machine it was proved on that request was **not honoured**: the Sandbox came up with a `Microsoft Hyper-V Network Adapter`, a default route, and a working internet. The first explanation was a **stale** sandbox - Windows Sandbox reuses a running virtual machine, and a live session keeps the configuration of the session it was started with. So a **fresh** sandbox was started, with nothing else running, and the adapter was **still** there, the route was **still** there, and the internet **still** answered. The setting is simply not honoured on that machine, and the proof cannot lean on it.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-156 | the offline proof starts from a **fresh** sandbox and says **which of the two** took the internet away | locked | A proof nobody can repeat is not a proof, and a proof that hides how it was reached cannot be trusted either. **Fresh**: Windows Sandbox **reuses a running virtual machine**, and a live session keeps the configuration of the session it was started with - so the proof is started by `installer/offline/run-offline-proof.ps1`, which **stops any Windows Sandbox that is running**, waits for it to be **gone**, writes the `.wsb` for the folder the payload is in (the shipped `.wsb` names one machine's path), starts it, waits for the result and **prints** it. **Honest**: the run **records the network as the Sandbox set it up, before it touches anything** - the adapters, the default route, the proxy - and then says, in words, **which of the two took the internet away**: the `.wsb` (no default route, the internet did not answer) or the run itself. The `.wsb` is kept, because it is the **right** setting, but nothing rests on it: the run takes the network down from inside by **three separate means** - an outbound firewall rule, the **default route removed**, and the **adapter disabled** - and reports **how many of them worked**, because one of them failed on the machine this was proved on. Only then is the machine **probed three ways** - a DNS lookup, a raw TCP connection to a public address, and an HTTP request - and if **any** of them answers, the run says the proof is **not valid** and the setup is not run at all. The proof only ever claims what it measured. |
| D-260 | undoing a page delete brings the page's elements back | locked | Deleting a page, clearing it, adding one and scaling its content are **recorded in the history** together with the page list, so undo puts back the page **and** every element and file that was on it, in the same order and place; redo does the same again. (The page list was recorded and the elements were not, so a teacher who undid a delete got the page back with its strokes missing.) |
| D-261 | the view can leave the page, and one button brings it back | locked | The camera is **not bound** to the page: the scroll wheel, the Hand tool (key `H`) and a drag move the view to any content on the scene, also outside the page's rect. A **"Back to this page"** button sits in the page bar and appears whenever the middle of the view is outside the current page; one click frames the page the way opening it does. Amends D-28 and D-117, which bound the camera to the page. |
| D-262 | a code card taller than the page is offered the split | locked | Inserting a document as a code card (the Insert button, the armed paste and a drop) raises the **same offer** as a paste that does not fit (D-24) when the card has more rows than the current page holds: **place what fits and keep the rest on the clipboard**, **keep it all on this page**, or **split it over pages** (a new page is added after the last one for each further run of rows). A card that fits is placed as it always was, and a document with no pages system is not offered anything. A card kept on one page that is taller than the page is reached by scrolling (D-261). |
| D-263 | text selected in the viewer can go onto the page as a card or a table | locked | Selecting text in the document viewer still copies it (D-19). A bar now appears above the document with **As a code card** and **As a table**: the first puts the selected text on the page as a code card (the one the Insert button builds), the second recovers the cells of the text and draws them as a table near the top-left of the view; text with no cells says so and places nothing. The bar is gone when nothing is selected and when the reader opens another document. The canvas context menu's "Reformat as..." (D-21) is unchanged. |
| D-268 | Python runs in the browser when the bridge is not connected | locked | The Run control of a code panel works **with no bridge** (D-32): the draft runs as Python in **Pyodide**, fetched from its CDN **the first time Run is pressed** (never on page load, and not an npm dependency), in a **worker**, so a program that never ends is stopped at the same **10 second** limit as the extension's run without freezing the page, and its output is cut at **20,000 characters**. The output, the end of the run and any notice reach the panel as the same messages the extension sends. The clock does not run while the runtime is downloading. A language other than Python says that it needs the bridge and runs nothing; a failed download says so, with the reason, and the next Run tries again. The panel's note says that Python runs in the browser. |
| D-264 | the presenter follows the editor off the page | locked | The page-relative camera the presenter mirrors is **not bounded** (D-261): it was capped at one page past the page's near edge, so when the editor scrolled down a long page the presenter stopped at a hard limit and stayed there. The offset is now the plain signed ratio in both directions, and the presenter shows the place the editor shows. Amends D-28. |
| D-267 | a file that could not be added says why | locked | When a dropped file or video cannot be added, the message names the file **and the reason** the browser gave (for example the storage quota), and when there is no reason it names the file only. "Could not add" with nothing else left the teacher guessing. |
| D-270 | the code editor's look: size, themes, languages | locked | The editor of a code panel draws its text at what used to be **200%**, now shown as **100%**, on a ladder of 50, 75, 100, 125, 150, 200, 300 and 400%. A **theme picker** offers **Dark** and **Light** (D-282) and the choice is kept in the panel. The editor **reads its text as Python** until the teacher chooses another language, so it has colours from the first line; the language list holds every grammar the app registers - JavaScript, TypeScript, Java, Python, C and C++ (**Rust is not offered**) - and the ones that run in the browser say so. There is **one language picker**: the language that is run is the one it shows, and choosing another changes what is run. |
| D-271 | a panel can be shown full screen | locked | Every code panel (the editor and the VS Code mirror) has a **full screen** button. It uses the browser's own full screen **on the panel's element**, so only the panel is shown, over the whole screen at its own size and without the canvas, and Esc leaves. |
| D-272 | Run works in the browser for Python and JavaScript | locked | Amends D-268: with no bridge, Run runs **Python** (Pyodide) and **JavaScript**. JavaScript runs in a **worker of its own for each run**, so a program that never ends is stopped at the 10 second limit and nothing it does stays behind; its console goes to the output, an error ends the run with its message. A program that **prints without end** cannot freeze the page: the worker stops sending past the output limit, the page writes what it gets to the panel a few times a second and **stops the program** when the limit is reached (Python and JavaScript alike). Go, Java, C and C++ are run from language packs (downloaded once, lazily) in a later step, and until then say that they need the bridge. |
| D-274 | the output console can be docked, zoomed and resized | locked | The program's output and the bridge's messages sit in a **console** that the teacher can dock **under** the editor or to its **right**, with a **draggable divider** between them (the console always keeps between 10% and 80% of the panel) and its **own zoom** (the editor's ladder) apart from the editor's. The text of the console is larger than before (16 px at 100%), a **clear** button empties it (the connection line stays), and the bars' controls are **icon buttons** one and a half times the size of the chrome's, so they can be pressed at any canvas zoom. The place, the size and the zoom are kept in the panel. |
| D-275 | code lands in the middle of the page, and the view goes there | locked | A code card inserted with no place asked for (the Insert button, a card from the viewer) is put in the **middle of the current page** - not the middle of whatever part of the canvas is in view, which may be far from the page - and the view **moves to it** with `scrollToContent`. A card taller than the page starts at the page's top. A card that is asked to go to a place (a paste under the pointer, a drop) goes there and the view stays. After the split offer (D-262) the view goes to the first part. |
