The command _Show where the mirror's time goes_ then says how long keys took to show - typically and at worst, how many took a second or more - separately for keys sent while the editor was in front and while it was behind other windows, and its sentence says so: slow behind and fast in front is _the editor draws late when it is not in front_; slow both ways is _being in front made no difference_. An older helper that says four numbers is still read. |
| D-372 | the live picture is **shown the moment it is sent**, not when the next one arrives | locked | The teacher: _"the number I type shows seconds late in the mirror, and at once when I Alt-Tab to the real window"_ - while the extension's own numbers (D-370, D-371) said that every key reached a changed picture in 100-140 ms. The cause is in the stream's format. The live view is a multipart image (`multipart/x-mixed-replace`) in an `<img>`, and **Chrome shows a part only once it has seen the start of the next part**: a boundary closing the part does not do it, and neither does a `Content-Length`. A still editor sends no next picture - only the keep-alive, five seconds later - so the picture with the typed digit stayed unseen until something else changed; Alt-Tab changes the picture, which is why it "fixed" it. A test with a small server and Chrome showed it exactly: with the old format the red picture sent at 0 s appeared at 3 s, when the blue one was sent, and the blue at 9 s - and with pictures of a megabyte as well; with the next part's header already open after each picture, each appeared in 20-170 ms. So the stream is now the pictures one after the other, **each part's boundary and header written already when the picture before it ends** (`--frame`, `Content-Type: image/jpeg`, an empty line, the bytes, then the next part's boundary and header at once). The per-part `Content-Length` and `X-Seq` go: the browser's `<img>` cannot read them, and the picture's size and number travel on the snapshot and the websocket. |
| D-373 | a new session starts from **`docs/next.md`**, the project's work queue, and it is private | locked | The owner, after wave 9a: _"write the next waves somewhere on what a new session should start working on"_, with the order of the coming waves changed twice in one conversation and a long list of ideas still to come. The method already says how a project hands work on: the kit's own `CONTINUE-TINS-PROJECT` prompt tells a new session to read the project's handover file and take the next item of its **work queue** (numbered items with a status). This repository's `HANDOVER.md` is the 2026-10-19 handover of the move to another harness and holds no queue, and the installed kit (`.tins/kit`) never points a session at any such file. So the project keeps **`docs/next.md`**: how to resume ("Continue: read docs/next.md"), where things stand (the private `main` commit, what the public repository holds, what the last wave left behind), the **work queue** in order (each item a `###` heading that ends in ` - DONE`, ` - NEXT`, ` - HELD` or ` - DROPPED`), an ideas inbox the owner fills, and the rules that always apply. A session that merges a wave updates it in the same task. **It is private**: it names the method, the owner's plans and their ideas, so `scripts/public-sync/config.json` denies it and the mirror never carries it. `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md`, which every session reads first, say to read it first. The kit itself has no mechanism for this (no key in `tins.json`, no line in the protocol file, nothing in `kit status`); the proposal to add one is staged in the kit's inbox and is the kit owner's to take, not a change made to the installed copy. |
| D-374 | a chart's animation can be **exported** as a GIF, an animated WebP or an MP4 | locked | The owner, with a phone recording of an infographic whose arrows have dashes running along them: _"can such gifs / mp4 be created using whatever we will build in wave 9b?"_ The flow studio already plays that animation (the **Marching dashes** arrow style); it could only be watched. The animation bar gets an **Export** button next to Play, which opens a panel. What is exported is the chart that is playing, or selected: its steps, its arrows and their labels, and every element it was made with - and nothing that lies next to it on the page. The run is **every step held for its time, once round** (four steps of 1.2 s are 4.8 s), never more than 60 seconds, and the panel says its length, the number of pictures and the size before anything is made. Each picture is made **from its moment, not from the screen**: the step that is current and how long it has been current come from the animation's own clock (a step is held for its time, the steps loop), and the marks that run along the arrows come from the very function the overlay draws with, so the same moment is always the same picture, on any machine, and nothing depends on how fast the machine is. A picture holds the chart as the canvas draws it, the marks of the arrow style (beam, marching dashes, pipe, token) in the look's colour and glow, and the highlight of the current step's shape (focus or pulse). What the look says about the arrows and the text is drawn too (D-383). The view that flies to each step is the panel's **Camera** choice (D-385). A picture is of the chart, not a recording of the screen. |
| D-375 | the export has a **frame-rate selector**, a size, a shape and a background | locked | The owner: _"we can include the fps selector"_. The selector offers 5, 10, 15, 24, 30 and 60 pictures a second; a GIF has no 60 (it offers up to 30), a GIF and a WebP start at 15 and an MP4 at 30. The **size** is the longest side of the picture: 480, 720, 1080 or 1920 pixels. The **shape** is the chart's own, 16:9, square or portrait 9:16 (a phone's), and the chart is placed whole and centred with a margin of 4% on every side; both sides of the picture are even numbers, as video needs. The choices are remembered in the browser, and a stored value that is not a choice is read as the default. |
| D-376 | the background is the page's own, or none - and an MP4 cannot have none | locked | The owner asked for the export _"with / without background of the canvas"_. **On the page's background** draws the canvas's background colour and the page's paper (D-243) under the chart. **Without** is transparent in a **WebP** (full alpha) and in a **GIF** (one bit of it: a pixel is transparent or it is not, with no soft edge). An **MP4** cannot hold transparency, so it is always on the page's background: the choice is shown ticked and cannot be changed, with the reason beside it. |
| D-377 | the files are written by **our own encoders**, with no new dependency | locked | The repository has no GIF, WebP or MP4 writer, and a library is not added for this. The **GIF** encoder takes its colours (at most 256, with an entry kept for the transparent pixels) from pictures spread over the run - two for each step, so the highlight of every step is among them, and never fewer than eight or more than twenty-four, writes each picture as it is made, loops for ever, and gives each picture a delay in hundredths of a second chosen so that the **total is exact** (15 a second is 7, 6, 7, 7, 6 ...). The **WebP** is the browser's own still WebP of each picture, put into the animation container (header, loop, one frame chunk each, with its time in milliseconds). The **MP4** is the browser's own H.264 video encoder (WebCodecs) with the pictures written into the container by a small writer, the tables before the pictures so it plays from its start; the codec level is chosen from the picture's size and rate, a key picture comes twice a second, and the video is silent. Each was checked by the real programs that read such files, not only by our own readers. |
| D-378 | **MP4 and WebP are offered only after the browser has been asked** | locked | The owner: _"optional mp4 after checking compatibility"_. Not every browser can write them (a Chromium without the proprietary codecs has no H.264 encoder; a browser may not write WebP from a canvas). When the panel opens it asks: whether the canvas writes WebP, and whether the video encoder supports H.264 at the chosen size and rate. A format the browser cannot write has its Export button **disabled and says why** ("This browser cannot write H.264 video at 1080x1920, 60 a second."), and the asking is done again whenever the size or the rate changes. GIF needs nothing of the browser and is always offered. |
| D-379 | an export **shows its progress, can be stopped and says what went wrong** | locked | The export makes up to 1800 pictures, so the page must not freeze and must not be unable to stop: between pictures it lets the page breathe, the panel shows a progress bar and a Stop, and a stop is honoured before the next picture and writes no file. Everything else in the panel is locked while it works. An export that cannot run (no steps, no length, more than 1800 pictures: _"That is 3600 pictures; the most is 1800. Choose a lower frame rate."_) says so before a picture is drawn. A failure is said in words, and the panel can try again. The file is downloaded under the chart's name with its own ending, and the panel says its size and its length in KB. |
| D-380 | the Flow tab's guide has a **worked example of an animated chart** | locked | The owner: _"include an example of this type in the guide of the flow tab"_. The guide opens with **An animated gateway chart**: a network script of callers, two gateways and the services behind them (ten boxes, eight arrows), the four steps from the script to a file (use the example, insert the chart, play it, export it), and a button **Use this animated example**. The button puts the script in the box, chooses the Network kind, and sets the animation of the arrows to **Marching dashes** and of the shapes to **Focus**, so that Play shows the dashes at once. All of it is in the code: nothing is fetched. |
| D-381 | the **drafts of the public texts** say `v1` and tell how the editor's window is handled today | locked | The drafts of the public README and feature list called the first release's branch `version-1`, and described only the virtual display - the way the window was handled before the canvas could control it. The branch is `v1` (its draft is `README.v1.md`), and the READMEs of the current line and the list of features now say: the canvas can **control** the window (three views: whole, part, follow), without bringing it forward where that works; the window can be **parked** or moved onto the **virtual display** and brought back; the virtual display should have the **same Windows scale** as the main one; a window **cannot be lost** (it is brought back, found again at start, and a rescue script reaches it). They contain no word of the method and no row number, so they can be published as they are. |
| D-382 | an **animated WebP** animates on the canvas, as a GIF does | locked | The owner, with the WebP the export had written: _"we don't currently support webp format in the canvas"_. A still WebP was already an image on the canvas, but an **animated** one was shown as its first picture, because the canvas keeps a GIF moving by playing it in the page (a DOM image, since only a visible image advances) and only a GIF was known to move. A WebP is now known to move **when its own header says so**: it is a WebP file whose extended header (`VP8X`) has the animation flag, read from the first 33 bytes of the file or of its data URL; a still WebP, a WebP with the header of a transparent still, and any other file are not. An animated WebP is then played, wherever an image is on the canvas - dropped, pasted, from the Files tab, or in the node of a chart. **It is not resized**: an image larger than the size limit is redrawn smaller on insertion, and redrawing an animation gives one picture; a GIF and an animated WebP are kept as they are (the limit on the file's size still holds). This also mends a GIF larger than the limit, which used to arrive as one picture. |
| D-383 | the export draws **the look** of the animation: its arrows and its text | locked | The owner, after the export was first built, asked whether the limits it was written with could be overcome. Four of the five can: **an arrow the look hides** is not drawn, and **one it colours** is drawn in that colour - given to the canvas by the same rule the live animation uses (a copy with another colour, never the element on the page); **the arrows as neon tubes** are drawn over the picture - a thin bright core in the arrow's colour (the mark's, and the accent when there is none) with three halos - and the plain arrows are then not drawn; **the highlight of the text** follows the look's target: the shape's outline, the rectangle on the label's box, or the label's letters drawn again in the highlight's colour (the text's, then the shape's, then the accent), with the label's own words, font, size and place, grown a margin by a focus and left alone by a pulse, and with the neon look's bright core and glow when it is on; a step with no label takes the shape, as the live animation does. The flight of the camera is a choice of its own (D-385). |
| D-384 | the file's **first picture is lit** | locked | The owner, asked whether the first picture of a file should already show the highlight, so that a thumbnail or a viewer that shows one picture shows more than a plain chart: _"yes first picture"_. A highlight comes in over 0.4 s, so the first picture of a run that starts at the start of the first step is the chart with nothing lit - which is what a viewer that does not play the file showed, and made the owner think the GIF had no animation. The run now **starts 0.4 s into the first step** (half the step, when a step is shorter than 0.8 s) and goes **one whole round** from there. A round of an animation that loops is the same wherever it starts, so the file ends where it began and loops with no jump; only the picture it is known by changes. |
| D-385 | the export can **fly the camera** to each step | locked | The owner, who asked for the flight of the camera to be added to the export panel: _"what about camera flight, add that to the export panel as well"_. The panel has a choice **Fly the camera to each step**, which starts as the bar's own Camera setting and is the export's alone (it does not change the bar). The picture then looks at the chart through a camera that flies from the step before to the step that is current - the live flight's own target for a step (the step's box with its padding, as large as the picture allows), the same easing - and the picture is drawn **again for every camera**, so the chart is as sharp as the camera is near. The live flight takes a second and a half and begins wherever the view is, so a step shorter than that is never reached and its path depends on the steps before it; a file cannot be like that. Here each flight **finishes inside its step** (it takes the shorter of a second and a half and the step) and starts from the step before (the last, for the first): the camera at any moment is a function of that moment alone, the same moment is the same picture, and the file loops. The camera does not leave the chart, with its padding. |
| D-386 | the panel **says when the picture would be too narrow** to read | locked | Found by exporting charts of fifty and a hundred boxes, each with a picture in it: in the chart's own shape a hundred boxes in a tree are a picture 92 pixels wide and 720 tall, with each box about six pixels - a file that is right and no use. When the picture would have a side shorter than 240 pixels, and the camera is not on, the panel says so under the line that gives the size: how big it would be, that the chart is very tall or very wide, and what to do - fly the camera to each step, choose a larger size, or choose 16:9, square or portrait. It is a word, not a refusal: the export is still offered. |
| D-387 | a file that **grows past half a gigabyte is stopped**, and it is said | locked | A minute of a large chart at a high rate is a thousand pictures or more, and the encoders hold the file in the page while they make it: the largest made in the test of fifty and a hundred boxes was 55 MB, and a larger size and rate could be many times that, which ends the page and loses the person's work. Each format counts what it has made - the GIF its bytes, the WebP its pictures, the MP4 what the encoder gives - and past **512 MB** the export stops with _"The file has grown past 512 MB, so the export was stopped. Choose a smaller size, a lower frame rate or a shorter chart."_ and writes nothing. |
| D-388 | the **Edit chart** window has the export too | locked | The owner, after the test with big charts: _"add these options into the edit as well"_. The Edit chart window already has the choices that decide how a chart fits its page - **Fit to page, Spread over pages, Allow larger than page** - beside the kind of chart and the shape; it now also has **Export animation**, beside Apply, which opens the same panel inside the window - the format, the frames a second, the size, the shape, the background and the camera, with the same checks (D-378) - for the chart that is being edited: its own elements, whatever is selected on the page. A chart with nothing to animate says so. |
| D-389 | a step's box is **the size its elements have now**, and the bar reads the page again when it changes | locked | The owner, after trying the camera: _"the fly to each step only considers the size of the node the first time it was created and does not care if I change its dimensions or shape later"_. A step's box was read when the chart was read, and the bar kept that reading while the list of elements was the same list - a node resized in place changes the scene but not the list. The bar now reads the chart again when the scene changes (its nonce is part of what the reading depends on), and the export takes each step's box from the bounds of that step's elements as they are when the export starts, so the camera centres on the node that is there. |
| D-390 | the export panel has its own **Nodes, Arrows and Step** choices, and says when nothing would move | locked | The owner: _"I had not set any animations, but the export has it"_. The export used whatever the bar was set to, and nothing in the panel showed it. The panel now has the highlight of the nodes, the animation along the arrows and the length of a step (100 ms to 60 s), starting from the bar's settings and changing the export only - never the bar. With no highlight, nothing along the arrows, no camera and no GIF that plays, it says that the file would be one picture and what to choose. |
| D-391 | a **GIF or an animated WebP in a chart plays in the file** | locked | The owner: _"my GIFs are only stuck at the first frame"_. The canvas draws whichever frame the browser holds, so the file had one frame (or, with the camera, frames at random). Each such file is now read frame by frame with the browser's own `ImageDecoder` - the length of each frame, a frame of next to no length is a tenth of a second as in a browser - and each picture of the file shows the frame that belongs to its moment, the file looping by its own length. The frame stands in for the file as a still picture (`<id>@<frame>`) while that picture is drawn; the page's elements are not touched; the canvas keeps the pictures it has loaded from one picture to the next; a chart is drawn again only when the camera or a frame has changed. Where the browser has no `ImageDecoder`, or a file cannot be read or has one frame, it stays one picture and the panel says so. |
| D-392 | the bar has **Select arrows** | locked | The owner could not select several arrows with Ctrl+click, to take off the arrowheads or make them dashed all at once. Shift+click adds to a selection and Ctrl+click is the deep select, which stays as it is; the bar beside a chart now has a **Select arrows** button that selects every arrow of the chart and nothing else, so the Arrowheads and Stroke style choices apply to all of them. |
| D-393 | the bar has **Select nodes** too | locked | The owner, after trying Select arrows: _"add select nodes as well, that will complete all the options"_. Beside **Select arrows** the bar has **Select nodes**, which selects every node of the chart with its words and its pictures and no arrow - also when what is selected now is only the arrows - so the colour, the shape or the size of all of them can be changed at once. |
| D-394 | the camera **centres a step that is not on the page** | locked | The owner, after the resize fix, with a recording of a chart that lies across the page's edge: _"the camera issue is only partially solved"_. The flight to a step (D-170) was always put through the page clamp, which keeps the view on the page and, where the page is smaller than the view on an axis, centres the **page** - so a node dragged or made larger past the page's edge, or below it, was left to one side. On an axis where the step itself (not its padding) is not inside the page, the flight now centres the **step**; a step inside the page is kept on the page exactly as before (the page clamp, and the zoom floor, still apply). The export's camera is unchanged: its page is the chart itself. |
| D-395 | the animation bar is in **rows**, and wraps before the editor's edge | locked | The owner: _"make the floating window of the animation multi-row so it is not a single row as it is right now"_. The first row held a dozen buttons and ran past the editor's edge when a panel was docked beside it. The buttons are now in three rows by what they do - **playing** (Play, Present steps, Stop while it runs, and the collapse button), **the chart** (Lay this out, Reset layout, Select arrows, Select nodes) and **passing it on** (Export, Edit chart, Look) - above the rows of choices, and the bar is given the width of the editor minus its place as its most, so a row wraps there and never reaches past it. The height the bar is placed with is the new one. |
| D-396 | the **exported camera is the one the view has**: every step in the middle | locked | The owner, comparing the two: _"the exported fly the view is not the same as what it shows me when I run it myself"_. The view flies to a step and puts it in the middle, and has the room of its page round the chart to do it for the first step and the last. The file's camera was held to the chart and its padding, so the first and the last step - and any step at the edge of a wide or tall chart - arrived at the edge of the picture. The file has no page, so its camera is held to nothing: the step is always in the middle of the picture, with the same zoom, the same padding and the same ease as the view (D-385 is otherwise as it was: from the step before, arriving inside the step, the same moment the same picture). What lies beyond the chart is the background, or nothing where it is left out. |
| D-397 | the assistant's tools are an **MCP server**, a separate program that the page connects to | locked | The owner, 2026-10-31: _"i want all functionality ... creation, editing ... deletion, modifying existing structures"_ and, after seeing the list of what the editor offers, _"ok i think thats the exhaustive list"_. The assistant's client (Claude, or another) starts a small program over **stdio** - `packages/mcp-server`, built into one file by `yarn build:mcp` and using only `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` (and the `zod` it asks for) - and it cannot reach into the page, so the **page connects to it**, as it connects to the VS Code bridge (D-50, D-67): the server listens on **127.0.0.1** only, on a fixed port (**47711**, `EXCALIDRAW_MCP_PORT` overrides it), the **origin is checked before the upgrade** and only a page served from this machine may connect, it is written on `node:http` and the bridge's own frame codec with **no websocket dependency**, and **one page is paired at a time** - a page that pairs later takes over. The tools are defined **once**, in `packages/excalidraw/mcp/toolSpecs.ts` (name, description, schema, whether it changes the scene); the server lists them and sends each call to the page, the page's runner (`mcp/runner.ts`) must have a handler for each, and a tool cannot exist on one side only. A call has a limit of 60 seconds and ends with an error the protocol names: not-connected, unknown-tool, bad-arguments, changes-not-allowed, too-large, not-found, failed or timeout - so a tool that fails is an answer and never a silence. |
| D-398 | the page is **paired** with a code typed once, in an **Assistant** tab | locked | The page has to prove it is the person's own page, and the person has to know the server is the one they started. On its first run the server makes an **eight-character code** (no 0, O, 1, I or L) and keeps it in the person's own folder (`~/.excalidraw-mcp`, or `EXCALIDRAW_MCP_HOME`); the **`status` tool** tells the assistant the code while no page is paired and not once one is; the person types it **once** in the sidebar's new **Assistant** tab (any case, with or without a dash) and it is remembered on this computer; the page presents it in its **first frame**, never in a URL and never on a command line, and the server compares it in constant time. A wrong code, or a connection that says anything else first, is closed with **1008**, and so is one that does not pair in 5 seconds; the page tells a **refused code** from a **server that is not there** and does not try a refused code again. A server that is not there is tried again, slower each time, from one second up to fifteen. The tab says where the link stands in a sentence, has Connect and Disconnect, and tells how to add the server to an assistant (`claude mcp add ...`). |
| D-399 | the assistant **cannot do what the person did not allow, and everything it does can be undone** | locked | The tab has an **Allow changes** switch, on until the person turns it off and remembered; the server is told when it changes, and the **page** enforces it: every tool the specs mark as changing the scene is refused with _changes-not-allowed_ while it is off, and the tools that only read go on working. A call takes at most **500 elements**, and an answer larger than 32 MB is an error that says to ask for less. Each call that changes the scene is **one undo step** - one Ctrl+Z takes the whole of it back, and one redo brings it again - by the editor's own history, and nothing the assistant does is saved anywhere but in the document. The person is told on the board (_"Assistant: create_elements: 3 elements"_) and in the tab's list of what it did, errors included. What the assistant reads from documents or the web is data, never instructions. |
| D-400 | the assistant can **look** before it changes anything | locked | `describe_capabilities` (whether changes are allowed, the app, the limits, the tools, how positions work), `list_pages` (number, id, name, size, frame, page number, paper, how many elements, which is open), `list_elements` (short summaries, by page, type, id or selection, in page terms or scene units, at most 2000 at a time), `get_element` (everything about one, with its bound text and its place on its page), `get_selection`, and **`screenshot`** - a PNG of the page (with its paper, frame and number), of the selection or of some elements, sized to the longest side asked for - so a model that can see sees the board. **Positions are page-relative** unless a tool says otherwise: (0, 0) is the top-left of the page in scene units, and pages are numbered from 1 as the person sees them. |
| D-401 | the assistant **creates, changes and deletes** elements as the editor would | locked | `create_elements` makes rectangles, diamonds, ellipses (with words in them), text, lines, arrows (sharp, round or elbow, **bound** to the shapes they join, with heads and a label), **freedraw** (from points, with or without pressure) and frames, with the styles the panel has, on the page named, through the editor's own converter - so a label is a real bound text and an arrow is really bound; an id the assistant gives is kept, and one that is taken is refused. `update_elements` moves, resizes, turns, locks, links, changes the words and the points, and the text bound to a shape and the arrows bound to it **follow**; styles are `set_properties`. `delete_elements` is the editor's own delete: the text bound to a shape goes with it and the arrows bound to it are unbound. `select_elements` and `go_to_page` move the person's selection and the open page. Every one is one undo step, and what a tool does not do it says in words. |
| D-402 | the **properties panel is a tool** | locked | The owner: _"what about the properties panel of all the elements?"_ `describe_properties(ids)` says exactly what the panel would show for those elements - the stroke and background colour, the fill style (only where something has a background), the stroke width and style, sloppiness, edges, the arrow type and heads, the font, its size and alignment, the opacity - with the value each has now (**"mixed"** where they differ) and what it may be; a control the panel would not show is left out, by the panel's own rules. `set_properties(ids, {...})` changes them **by the panel's own actions** on the elements named as though they were selected, so the side effects are the panel's (a font size re-fits the text in its shape, an arrowhead keeps the arrow bound), all of it is **one undo step**, **the person's own selection is put back** and the style of the next elements they draw is not changed. A property the panel would not show, a value it does not offer, a colour that is not a colour and a name it does not have are refused in words, and nothing is changed by a call that is refused. |
| D-403 | the assistant **manages pages** | locked | The owner asked for pages, page frames and numbers, and sizes (_"what about background, pages and page frames, page numbers"_). `add_page` (after or before a number, with a name; it does not open the page unless asked), `rename_page`, `move_page`, `delete_page`, `clear_page`, `list_deleted_pages` and `restore_page`, `set_page` (the frame, the page number, a size from the presets - which the page remembers - or a width and height that are never below 1920 by 1080, and the paper) and `scale_page_content` (half the size, or back by two). A page's place is positional and what is on it lives in scene coordinates, so **every change of the page list or of a page's size moves the content of the pages that moved** with them, as the page bar does; a deleted page's elements are soft-deleted, so one undo brings the page back with what was on it, and the page is also kept in the app's own store (D-280) so it comes back after a reload - a store that fails never stops a delete. Every one is one undo step. |
| D-404 | the assistant sets the **look of a page** and the **ink** | locked | The owner: _"what about page looks?"_ `describe_page_look` says what a page may be - the eight paper types, the twelve paper colours or the canvas, grain and age from 0 to 100, the size presets, and the four one-click looks (old paper, green chalkboard, blackboard, neon night) with the ink each brings - and what it is now. `set_page` sets a paper (what is not given stays what the page has; a type, colour or number out of range is refused), on every page when asked. `apply_page_look` does what the Page look dialog does: the page's paper, and **the ink and colour the person's next elements are drawn with**, while what is drawn already is not recoloured. `set_ink` changes the ink look of drawn elements (normal, neon, chalk, marker, pencil, an amount from 1 to 100, a colour) by the panel's own action, and leaves the ink the person draws with next as it was. |
| D-405 | the assistant controls the **canvas and the view**, searches, counts, exports and inserts | locked | `set_canvas`: the background colour (one undo step), the grid and its size, snapping to other elements and to midpoints, the theme, zen mode, view-only, the stats panel and the document's name. `set_view`: fit a page, the selection or some elements, a zoom, a point of a page - this moves what the person sees and never the document. `set_tool`: the tool the person draws with next. `find_text`: every text, and the shape it is in, with the words (and select them). `get_stats`: the numbers the stats panel shows - counts by type and by page, and where the selection is on its page and how large. `export_scene`: a page, the whole document or the selection as a PNG (an image), SVG or the saved JSON, at a scale from 0.25 to 4, with or without the background, dark or light; a file too large to give back is refused in words. `insert_document`: the text of a saved `.excalidraw` document is put on a page beside what is there, its elements and files with ids of their own, in one undo step (D-35 to D-38). |
| D-406 | **every action the editor registers is accounted for**, and the assistant can arrange, style and check | locked | The owner: _"i want everything the excalidraw offers to be controllable, editable and deletable"_. The editor registers about a hundred actions; `mcp/actionCatalog.ts` names **every one** - those `run_action` runs on the elements it is given (align, distribute, flip, group, layers, lock, bind a text, bold, bigger and smaller, polygon, frames, the grid and snapping), those another tool already does (all the panel's properties, delete, select, export, the view), those a later wave brings (flow charts, the crop and a panel's choices), and those that are not for an assistant with the reason (the clipboard, the person's undo and redo, emptying every page, file dialogs, editing modes, collaboration) - and **a test fails when an action is registered that the list does not name**, so a new feature cannot be left out. Besides: `duplicate_elements` (copies with their words and arrows, to where the assistant says), `style_text` (a part of a text bold or italic), `bind_arrow` (bind or unbind the ends of an arrow), `mermaid_to_diagram` (a diagram drawn from Mermaid text, as ordinary elements) and **`check_page`**, which tells what would not read well - things that reach outside the page, things that lie on each other, text under 18 tall, and text whose colour is too near the colour behind it (the shape it is in, or the paper) by the WCAG contrast ratio - with the ids, so the assistant can put it right. |
| D-407 | the assistant uses the **library, the stickers, tables and code cards** | locked | The owner named the emoji and GIF bar, and the editor's other builders. `library_list` and `library_insert` (the shape library: what it holds, and an item put on a page with its top-left where asked, with ids of its own; adding to it is the action `addToLibrary` through `run_action`). **The tools that need what only the app has** are defined with the others (`app: true` in the one list of tools) and handled by the app, which gives them to the runner: `list_stickers` (the whiteboard's own emoji and animated GIFs, found by words with the editor's own search, by kind), `insert_sticker` (a sticker by id on a page - top-left where asked, or the middle of the page, longest side as asked, a GIF stays a GIF), `insert_table` (rows of cell texts drawn as boxes and their texts in one group, never wider than the page, at most 60 rows and 12 columns and 500 characters a cell) and `insert_code_card` (code as the dark, highlighted card the Insert button builds, at most 20000 characters). They put the thing on **the page named** without moving the person's view or selection, in one undo step, and a build that does not have one says so. The count of tools that are the library's and the app's is checked by a test, so one cannot exist without a handler. |
| D-408 | the assistant reads and writes **only in the folders the person lists** | locked | The owner, planning the files: _"insertion of external resources like images, gif, videos from the local filesystem"_ and _"only inside folders the owner lists; refuse any other path"_ (docs/next.md). The server is the one that touches the disk, so the rule is there: **nothing is allowed until a folder is listed** - in the arguments the assistant's client starts the server with (`--folder <path>`, as often as needed) or in `EXCALIDRAW_MCP_FOLDERS` - and a path is allowed only when what it **really is** lies inside one of them: a link or shortcut is followed first, so `..`, a link that leads out and a name that only looks inside all end where they end, and a name outside gets the **same answer whether the file is there or not**, so the assistant cannot find out what is on the rest of the disk. A file larger than its kind may be (pictures 25 MB, video 400 MB, documents 100 MB) is refused with the number; a file is made only inside the first folder and never over one that is there, with a name that is a name. `list_allowed_folders`, `list_folder` (a link that leads out is not shown) and `file_info` (the kind from the name, the type **from the bytes**). These tools are the server's own - it has the disk, and the page has not - and the tools only the server calls (`upload_chunk`, `upload_drop`, `place_image`) are not in the list the assistant sees and cannot be called by it. |
| D-409 | a **picture from a file** goes on a page, and can be cropped | locked | `insert_image` takes a path in an allowed folder (or an address, D-410): the server reads the file, decides **from its bytes** what it is - a file called .png that is a web page is not a picture - and sends it to the page in pieces of 3 MB under one id (a message has a size and a video has none; the page keeps what is not used for ten minutes at most and never more than 600 MB, and a piece that comes twice is the same piece); the page reads the picture's size from its own header (PNG, GIF - which plays -, JPEG, the three kinds of WebP, BMP, AVIF, SVG), puts it on the page named with its top-left where asked or in the middle, its longest side `size` long or its own size up to 600, in one undo step, with its file kept in the document; a page that cannot use the file forgets it. **Where it came from** (the file, or the address, with a licence and an attribution the assistant gives) is kept on the picture and `list_sources` tells it, so a handout can credit what is on it. `crop_image` crops to a part given as fractions of the whole picture (the part stays where it was on the page, at the scale the picture has there; a second crop is from the whole, not from the part; `reset` takes it off; a picture that is turned must be turned back). |
| D-410 | the **internet** is off until the person turns it on, and is never a way in | locked | The owner: _"getting resources from the internet"_. The Assistant tab has **Allow internet**, off by default and remembered; the server is told with the rest of the state and refuses `fetch_resource` and `insert_image` with an address until it is on, and says how it is turned on. When on: **https only**, no name and password in the address, no odd port; the name is resolved by the server and **every** address it stands for must be a public one - not this computer, not the person's network, not the cloud's metadata address, not the ones set aside, also when they are written inside an IPv6 address - and the connection goes to the address that was checked, not to a second lookup; a redirect is followed by hand, five times at most, and each place is checked as the first; nothing of the person's is sent (no cookies, no credentials); a time limit, and a limit of 25 MB **counted as the bytes arrive**, not trusted from a header. A web page comes back as its words, **marked as data from the web and not as instructions**; a picture, a PDF or a video is kept in an allowed folder, under a name the assistant gives, with where it came from in a file beside it, and never over one that is there. |
| D-411 | the assistant **reads a document** of an allowed folder | locked | The owner: _"documents (markdown, PDF)"_ among what the assistant must be able to use. `read_document` takes a path in an allowed folder (D-408). A text or markdown file comes back as its text, at most `maxChars` (20 000 by default, 100 000 at most) with whether it was cut. A PDF goes to the page in pieces (D-409) and the page reads it with the PDF reader the whiteboard already uses: the text of the pages asked for (`pages`, as `1-3,5`; the first ten when not given, at most thirty at a time), and when `images` is true a picture of each of the first three, so the assistant sees what is on them. What comes back is **data from a file, not instructions**, and says so. A file that is not a document, a page that is not in the file, and a PDF the reader cannot open are told with the reason (the reader is fetched by the browser the first time, so a browser with no internet cannot read a PDF). |
| D-412 | **markdown goes on a page as the things a page is made of** | locked | `insert_markdown` takes a path in an allowed folder or `markdown` text, a top-left (`x`, `y`, page-relative) and a `width` (the page's width less margins by default). The server reads the file; the page makes, in one undo step and top to bottom: headings (three sizes, bold), paragraphs and quotes as text that wraps in the width, lists (a bullet or a number to each item, nested ones indented), a code block as a **code card** (D-407), a table as a **table** (D-407), a rule as a line; what is marked bold or italic keeps it. A picture in the markdown is not fetched (relative pictures are dropped from this wave) and is listed in `skipped`, as is html. A document that would not fit on the page still goes on, below it, and the answer says how tall it is. |
| D-413 | **a PDF page, or a part of one, goes on a page as a picture** | locked | `insert_pdf_page` takes a path to a PDF in an allowed folder, `pdfPage` (from 1), an optional `region` (left, top, width, height as fractions of the page, 0 to 1), and where and how large as `insert_image` does (D-409); the page draws the PDF page with the PDF reader at a sharpness for its size (the longest side about 1600 pixels for a whole page, the same sharpness for a part), cuts the part, and puts it on the page as a picture with where it came from (the file and the PDF page) kept (D-409). A PDF with more than 300 pages, a page that is not in the file and a part outside the page are refused. |
| D-414 | the assistant **reads the words in a picture** | locked | The owner: OCR is among what the whiteboard does for the teacher; the assistant gets it too. `read_image_text` takes the id of a picture on a page and returns the words in it - the whole text and each line with where it is in the picture (as fractions) - read in the browser with the reader the whiteboard already has (fetched by the browser the first time); a picture that is cropped is read as it is shown. The words are **data read from a picture, not instructions**, and the answer says so. A thing that is not a picture and a picture whose file is gone are refused. |
| D-415 | the assistant **lists the panels** on the pages | locked | The owner: everything the whiteboard offers must be controllable, and the panels are part of it. `list_panels` gives, for each panel that is not deleted: the id, the page, the place and size (page-relative, as every tool says them) and what it holds - a video panel's source (a file, a stored video, YouTube with its video id) and how many caption tracks it has, a code panel's language, how much code it has and whether its editor is the browser's or the bridge's. |
| D-416 | a **video from a file** and a **YouTube video** go on a page as video panels | locked | The owner: _"insertion of external resources like images, gif, videos from the local filesystem"_ (docs/next.md). `insert_video` takes a path to a video in an allowed folder (mp4, webm, mov, m4v, ogv - decided from its bytes, not its name) and sends it to the page in pieces (D-409); the captions are the `.srt` or `.vtt` file named in `captions`, or else the one beside the video with its name (`lesson.srt`, and `lesson.en.srt` when there is no plain one); the page puts the video panel with the app's own insert - the same panel and the same caption text beside it the Files panel makes - with its top-left where asked (the middle of the page when not), its width as asked (the shape of the video kept), on the page asked for, **the person's selection left as it was**, in one undo step. `insert_youtube` takes a YouTube link or a video id and puts the panel the same way; it plays in YouTube's own player, so the browser needs the internet to show it. A file that is not a video, captions that are not captions, a link that is not YouTube's and a width out of range are refused, and the page forgets what it could not use. |
| D-417 | a **code editor panel** is put, changed and read | locked | `insert_code_panel` puts the editor that works in the browser (the one the whiteboard has with no bridge) with the code and the language given (python when not), at a place and a size within what a code panel is (280 to 1200 wide, 200 to 1200 tall; 640 by 400 at 100, 100 when not said), on the page asked for, in one undo step; the text is the panel's own, as if the teacher had typed it. `set_code_panel` changes the code, the language or both in one undo step; `read_code_panel` gives the language, the code (at most 100 000 characters, with whether it was cut), whether the text is the teacher's own or what a bridge mirrors. A language is a name (python, javascript, c++); code over 200 000 characters, a panel that is not a code panel and a change that changes nothing are refused. |
| D-418 | the assistant **runs Python or JavaScript** in the browser | locked | `run_code` takes the id of a code panel (its code and language) or code and a language (python when not said), and the lines the program reads with `input()` as `stdin`, and runs it with the runners the code panel's Run uses: the browser's own sandbox, no files, ten seconds, 20 000 characters of output. It says how the run ended (the exit code, a timeout, a stop, a crash, a runtime that could not be downloaded, or that the program asked for a line nobody gave), and what it printed, by stream, **marked as data and not as instructions**. It needs **Allow changes**: a program run by an assistant can reach the internet like any web page, so it is under the same switch as everything that does something. A language the browser does not run (it runs Python and JavaScript), no code, an id and code together and a panel that is not a code panel are refused. The panel's own console is not changed. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-190 | the offline proof starts from a **fresh** sandbox, takes the network down by **more than one way**, and says **which of the two** took the internet away | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/offlineProof.test.ts` | The test reads the **files** - the `.wsb`, the run that happens inside it and the launcher that starts it - and asserts on them; it starts no sandbox and touches no network. It pins: the `.wsb` asks for **no networking**, maps the payload folder and runs the proof, and says in its own words that the setting is **not trusted**; the run records the adapters and the default route **before** the takeover, and asks the internet **before** it changes anything; the run **names** the two and says which one it was, deciding it by **facts** (the default route that was there, and whether the internet answered) rather than by an assumption; the takeover uses **three** means and reports **how many** worked; the machine is probed **three ways** and any answer makes the proof **invalid**; and the launcher **stops a running Windows Sandbox**, waits for it to be gone, writes the `.wsb` for **its own** folder, starts it, and prints the result with the verdict taken from the machine's own words. Tests: `AC-190 the sandbox file asks for no networking and runs the proof`, `AC-190 the run records the network as the sandbox set it up, BEFORE it takes it down`, `AC-190 the run says which of the two took the internet away`, `AC-190 the run takes the network down by more than one way, and says how many worked`, `AC-190 the proof is only valid when the machine is really offline`, `AC-190 the launcher starts a sandbox that is fresh, and prints the result`. |

### Wave 7p - the emoji and GIF bar

The owner asked for a bar like the pages bar that holds the emoji and GIFs people actually use, to **drag onto the canvas**, with a **search box** like the phone keyboard's - and **everything in the repo, nothing fetched from a third party**. Popular meme GIFs from Giphy or Tenor are somebody else's copyright and cannot be redistributed, so the assets are **our own**.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-157 | the stickers are **our own artwork**, committed as files, and nothing is fetched from a third party | locked | The emoji are **original simplified vector artwork** drawn from geometric primitives by `excalidraw-app/emoji/tools/build-stickers.mjs`, and the GIFs are **animated renderings of the same artwork** by the same tool, which carries its own GIF encoder - **no dependency is added**. The files are committed under `excalidraw-app/emoji/assets/`, so the bar works with no internet and no API key. They are dedicated to the public domain (CC0) and `docs/STICKERS.md` records the **provenance** of every file: what drew it, the tool, the date, the licence. A bundled set that names a third-party service, key or remote URL in any asset or in the code that loads it is a defect. The size is bounded - one file at most 60 KB and the whole set at most 1.5 MB - so the repo does not bloat. |
| D-158 | one **catalog**, one **pure search** | locked | Every sticker is one row in `excalidraw-app/emoji/catalog.ts`: `id`, `kind` (`emoji` or `gif`), `label`, `tags`, `file`, and its place in the **popularity order** (the order the bar shows with an empty search box). The search is a **pure function** in `excalidraw-app/emoji/search.ts` - it takes the catalog, a query and a kind filter and returns the matching rows - so it is tested without a DOM. The query is trimmed and lower-cased and split into words, **every** word must match the label or a tag, a **prefix** of a label or tag outranks a match in the middle of one, and ties keep the popularity order. An empty query returns the whole set in popularity order, and a query that matches nothing returns an empty list - never the whole set. |
| D-159 | the bar is an **island above the page bar**, with an **Emoji** tab, a **GIF** tab and a **search box** | locked | The bar uses the page bar's visual language (`--island-bg-color`, `--shadow-island`) and sits **above** it, centred in the space the sidebar leaves (D-121). It has two tabs, one search box that searches the open tab, and a scrolling row of stickers. It can be tucked away to a small handle, and **remembers** that (`localStorage`, wrapped in try/catch like the page bar's). A tab with no match says so in words. The search box is a real `input type=search` with a label, and every sticker is a button with an accessible name. |
| D-160 | a sticker lands on the canvas by **drag** or by **click**, on the **page the teacher is on**, and a GIF stays a GIF | locked | **Drag**: the sticker is draggable and carries its id under its own type (`application/x-excalidraw-sticker`); the canvas accepts the drop, **stops the library's own drop handling** for it, and places the sticker by its **centre** at the pointer, converted with the same scene maths the rest of the app uses. **Click**: the sticker lands in the middle of what is visible, clear of the sidebar. Either way it goes through `insertImageIntoScene`, so it is **stamped to the current page** (D-12) and a GIF keeps the `image/gif` type that the animated-image overlay plays. Its long edge is at most the app's image maximum. A drop that carries any other data is left to the library. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-191 | the bundled set is **complete, valid, small and offline** | `excalidraw-app/tests/stickerAssets.test.ts` | The test reads the **files**. Every catalog row has a file that exists; ids are unique; there are at least 40 emoji and at least 12 GIFs; every GIF is a real GIF89a with **more than one frame** and a loop; every emoji is well-formed SVG with a `viewBox`; **no asset or catalog row contains a remote URL** (the SVG namespace aside); the files are within the size bounds; and `docs/STICKERS.md` names every file. Tests: `AC-191 every catalog row has its file`, `AC-191 every gif is animated`, `AC-191 every emoji is a well-formed svg`, `AC-191 nothing names a remote url`, `AC-191 the set is within its size bounds`, `AC-191 the provenance names every file`. |
| AC-192 | the search finds what the teacher means, in the order they would expect | `excalidraw-app/tests/stickerSearch.test.ts` | Pure-function tests over the real catalog: an empty query returns the whole set in popularity order, a tag finds its sticker, every word must match, a prefix outranks a middle match, the kind filter holds, case and padding do not matter, and a query that matches nothing returns nothing. Tests: `AC-192 an empty query is the popularity order`, `AC-192 a tag finds its sticker`, `AC-192 every word must match`, `AC-192 a prefix outranks a middle match`, `AC-192 the kind filter holds`, `AC-192 case and padding do not matter`, `AC-192 nothing matches nothing`. |
| AC-193 | the bar shows the two tabs, searches the open tab, and tucks away | `excalidraw-app/tests/stickerBar.test.tsx` | Rendered with the editor: the Emoji tab is open first, the GIF tab shows only GIFs, typing narrows the row, a query with no match says so, and the hide control leaves a handle and is remembered across a remount. Tests: `AC-193 the emoji tab is open first and the gif tab shows only gifs`, `AC-193 the search narrows the open tab`, `AC-193 a search with no match says so`, `AC-193 the bar tucks away and remembers it`. |
| AC-194 | a click or a drop puts the sticker on the **current page**, a GIF stays a GIF, and nothing leaves the machine | `excalidraw-app/tests/stickerBar.test.tsx` | With the second page active: a click adds one image element **stamped to that page**; a GIF's file has the `image/gif` type; a drop lands the sticker's **centre** at the pointer, checked **through the library's own conversion** and not a re-derived formula; the library's own drop handling does not also run; a drop of other data is not claimed; and every request the bar makes is **same-origin**. Tests: `AC-194 a click lands on the current page`, `AC-194 a gif keeps its type`, `AC-194 a drop centres the sticker at the pointer`, `AC-194 a drop of other data is left to the library`, `AC-194 nothing leaves the machine`. |

### Wave 7q - the flow studio

The owner asked for a **fully featured creation tool** for flowcharts and tier charts, with **presets** that make an **animated flowchart, an interactive step-by-step process diagram, an animated integration, and node-and-edge flows** quick to make. Wave 6c already gave a selected flowchart a **focus overlay, a loop and an auto-layout** (D-42 to D-44, D-60 to D-63); this wave adds the part that is missing - **making** the chart - and the **animation styles** the owner pointed at. The references and what each one is (read on 2026-10-27): the **Animated Beam** (MagicUI) is a line of light travelling along a curved path between two things, with a curvature, a direction and a duration; **React Flow's animating edges** move a dot along an edge's path, or move a whole node along it; **JointJS's animated process flow** lights up the steps downstream of the one you click. The two CodePens were first refused (**403**) and were then **saved by the owner and read** on 2026-10-27: `Hekutoru/GYeEVV` is **"Process Flow GoJS"** - a process diagram whose pipes are three stacked strokes with a **dashed line that marches** along them - and `sdras/VjvGJM` is **"Animated Flow Chart to demo animating viewBox"** - a click-through decision tree where choosing **Yes** or **No** makes the **camera fly** to the next step and an end step is **drawn on** and then filled. D-168 records what is taken from each and what is not.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-161 | the studio is a **Flow tab in the sidebar**, and a chart is made of **ordinary elements** | locked | The presets live in a new **Flow** tab beside Files, in the app's sidebar, because that is where the teacher already goes to put things on the page. A chart is **ordinary shapes and arrows** - rectangles, diamonds and ellipses with their **text bound inside**, and arrows **bound at both ends** - exactly as D-42 says of the flowchart it animates: **no new element type**, so a chart can be edited, moved, exported, saved and undone like anything else, and the animation (D-166) plays over it without writing into it. A chart lands on the **page the teacher is on** (D-12), in the middle of what is visible, through the same `appendElements` the sticker bar and the sidebar use. |
| D-162 | a preset is a **pure builder**, in the library, with no DOM | locked | `packages/excalidraw/scene/flowPresets.ts` exports `buildFlowPreset(presetId, labels, options)`, which returns **elements** and touches no DOM, no `appState` and no network - so it is tested without a canvas, and the MCP server (a later wave) calls the **same** function the sidebar does. It lives in `packages/excalidraw` because that is the only package that may import dagre (D-62). Every shape it returns has its **bound text** and its `boundElements` naming the arrows; every arrow's `startBinding` and `endBinding` name **shapes that are in the result**, and the shapes' `boundElements` name the arrow back - both sides, or the chart comes apart the first time a shape is moved. Ids are **unique** and **seeded** by the caller, so the same input gives the same chart. |
| D-163 | the presets | locked | Seven: **process** (a straight run of steps), **decision** (a question with a yes branch and a no branch that rejoin), **steps** (a numbered step-by-step, each step carrying its number), **cycle** (a loop of steps that returns to the first - D-62 already says a loop is expected), **tiers** (stacked layers, each with its own items, for a tier chart or a layered architecture), **integration** (one hub with the things it connects to around it, for "our app talks to these services"), and **network** (nodes joined by edges, for a general node-and-edge flow). Each preset has a **name**, a one-line **description** and a set of **example labels**, so choosing one shows a chart that is already sensible and the teacher only changes the words. |
| D-164 | the chart is filled from a **script**: one line per node | locked | The studio's box takes **text, one line per node** - what a teacher already writes when planning a class. The rules are small and are the same for every preset: a blank line is ignored; a line is a **label**; in **tiers** a line is one tier and the items in it are separated by a vertical bar; in **integration** the **first** line is the hub and each later line is a spoke; in **decision** a line that starts with `?` is a question and the **next two** lines are its yes and no branches; in **network** a line of the form `A -> B` is an **edge** between two nodes that are created the first time their label is seen. A label is trimmed, a label that is empty after trimming is dropped, and a script with **no usable line** builds nothing and says so - it never builds an empty chart. |
| D-165 | the chart **fits the page** | locked | A graph preset (process, decision, steps, network) is laid out with the existing **`layoutFlow`** (dagre, D-62), top-to-bottom or left-to-right, **whichever fits the page better**; a **cycle** is laid out as a **ring**, because a loop reads as a loop only when it goes round, and a line with one long arrow back says something else; **tiers** and **integration** (the hub in the middle, the rest round it) are laid out by plain geometry. Whatever the number of nodes, the chart is **scaled to fit the page's rect** - never larger than the page and never off it - down to a **floor** on the text size (so it stays readable from the back of a room). A script with more nodes than fit at that floor is **reported to the teacher** and not silently shrunk past the floor, and **no two shapes overlap**. |
| D-166 | six **animation styles**, played as an overlay, written to nothing | locked | The wave 6c overlay (D-44) gains a **style** chosen in the properties-panel section (D-42): **focus** (what exists today), **beam** (a line of light travelling along each edge from the step to the next - the Animated Beam), **marching** (the edge's dashes move along it - the animated edge), **pipe** (marching, drawn as a pipe - D-171), **token** (a dot travels along the edge from one step to the next - React Flow's moving circle and JointJS's token), and **pulse** (the current step glows). The path a beam, dash or token follows is **the arrow's own geometry** - its points, taken from the element and converted with the library's own `sceneCoordsToViewportCoords` (D-34) - so it follows a curved or elbowed arrow and moves when the arrow moves. The clock is the `AnimationController` and never React state (D-63), nothing is written to the document or to `appState` (AC-51 still holds for every style; the camera flight of D-170 is the one thing that touches `appState`, it moves only the view, and it is off by default), and a style is **only** a way of drawing the overlay: the step order (D-43) is the same for all six. |
| D-167 | **interactive step-by-step**, and **play from here** | locked | The section gains **Present steps**: the flow does **not** loop; it shows the steps **reached so far** and dims the rest, and the **next** and **previous** controls - and the right and left arrow keys, which the overlay claims **only while it is presenting** - move one step. While presenting there is also **play from here**: it runs the **current step and everything downstream of it** once (the JointJS behaviour of clicking a node), in the step order, and returns to the canvas when it ends. Both are overlay-only, like every style, and leaving presentation restores the canvas exactly as it was. |
| D-168 | the two CodePen references, **read**: what is taken and what is not | locked | **Read, not copied**: both pens are somebody else's code (a Northwoods GoJS sample, and a pen by its author), so **none of their code, artwork or text is in this repository** - the wave takes the **ideas** and writes its own. **`GYeEVV` (Process Flow GoJS)** is a process diagram: each link is a **pipe** of three stacked strokes (black 5, grey 3, and a **white dashed line** `[10,10]`), and a 100 ms loop steps the dash offset by 2 so the flow runs along the pipe; it also has grid snapping, rotatable valve symbols, and links that route around nodes with a gap where two cross. **Taken**: the **pipe look** and its dash-offset flow (D-171). **Not taken**: GoJS itself, the valve and tank symbols (an engineering drawing's vocabulary, not a teacher's), the grid snap and the rotatable symbols - Excalidraw has its own snapping and a chart is made of its ordinary shapes (D-161). **`VjvGJM` (Animated Flow Chart)** is a click-through decision tree: clicking a **Yes** or **No** tweens the SVG `viewBox` for 1.5 s (ease-out) to the target step's bounds plus 75 units of padding - and for some branches through an **intermediate step first**, holding 1 s - and an **end** step has its outline **drawn on** stroke by stroke, then filled. **Taken**: the **camera that flies to the step** (D-170) and the **choose-your-path** flow with its **draw-on end** (D-172). **Not taken**: the artwork, the dark theme, the jQuery and TweenMax and DrawSVG plugins - the clock is the `AnimationController` (D-63), and nothing is added (D-169). |
| D-169 | nothing is added to the app but code | locked | No dependency, no font, no network request and no file larger than the code. The presets' example labels are **our own words**, and the shapes use the library's own fonts and colours, so the studio works with no internet. |
| D-170 | the **camera flies** to the step, and only the view moves | locked | While a flow is playing or being presented, the view **eases** to the current step: the target is the step's bounds (D-60, the container and its label together) **plus a padding** of 75 scene units - the number the reference pen uses - and the move takes **1.5 s** by default with an **ease-out** curve. The flight changes **only** `scrollX`, `scrollY` and `zoom` - the **view**, never an element and never the page structure - and the camera **stays on the page** (the page clamp, D-4, still applies, so a flight never leaves the page's rect and never goes below the zoom floor, AC-155). It is **off by default**, a switch in the section, because a teacher who is talking over a chart often wants the view to stay where it is. **Leaving** the flow restores the view to **exactly** where it was when the flow started. The tween's clock is the `AnimationController` (D-63). |
| D-171 | the **pipe** look | locked | A sixth style (D-166): each edge is drawn **as a pipe** - three strokes laid one over another, a **dark** outer stroke, a **lighter** inner stroke and a **dashed highlight** on top - and the highlight's **dash offset advances along the edge from the step to the next**, so the flow runs in the arrow's direction. The three strokes and the dash are drawn by the **overlay** along the arrow's own geometry (D-166); the arrow in the document keeps its own stroke and is not changed. The advance is **time-based** (a fixed speed in scene units per second), not frame-based, so a slow machine does not run the flow slower. |
| D-172 | **choose your path**, with a **draw-on** end | locked | In **present steps** (D-167) on a **decision** chart (D-163) the **branch shapes the teacher or the class picks**: while a question is the current step, its two branches are **clickable** in the overlay, and choosing one makes the **target of that branch** the next step - the flow follows the **choice**, not the step order. The step order (D-43) is still what `next` and `previous` follow when nobody chooses. When the next step is an **end** (a shape with no arrow leaving it), its outline is **drawn on** - the stroke grows from nothing to the whole outline over about 1.5 s - and then **filled**. Both are the overlay's: the end shape in the document is not changed, and leaving restores the canvas exactly (D-167). |
| D-173 | **resizing** a chart keeps its arrows on its shapes | locked | A chart (D-161) resized as a whole - the selection's handles, or the arrow keys with a modifier - keeps **every arrow bound at both ends** and **ending on the edge of its shape**: after the resize, no arrow end is off its shape, no binding names a shape that is gone, and a shape's `boundElements` still names its arrows (D-162's two-sided agreement still holds). The shapes' **text scales with them** and stays inside them. The fix is in the resize path that moves bound arrows, not a repair pass run after the fact, and a chart of the library's own presets is the case it is tested on. |
| D-174 | **lay this out** keeps the chart's scale, and can be **reset** | locked | The layout action (D-62) re-spaces a chart **without changing the size of its shapes or of their text**, so a chart laid out after it was resized keeps the size it had. The first time a chart is laid out, each shape's position is **remembered** on the shape (`customData.flow.home`, ordinary element data, so it saves, exports and undoes with the chart); a new action **reset layout** puts every shape back at its remembered position - arrows following, one undo step - and is offered only where there is something to reset. A shape that has been moved by hand since is **still reset**: reset means the remembered layout, not the last one. |
| D-175 | the animation's marks **scale with the chart** | locked | The beam's length, the dash lengths, the token's radius, the pipe's strokes and the pulse's glow are drawn in **scene units multiplied by a chart scale**: the chart's own text size (the median of its shapes' font sizes) divided by the preset's base size (24), kept between one half and four. A chart that is a hundred nodes at the floor font size gets **smaller marks** and a chart the teacher has resized up gets **larger ones**, and the same chart is drawn the same at any zoom. The scale is read from the elements each time the overlay draws, so it follows a resize at once. |
| D-176 | the **Flow tab fits any window** | locked | The tab is laid out as a **scrolling body with a footer that does not scroll**: the preset choices and the script take the room that is left and scroll inside it, and the **Insert chart** button and the message under it are **always visible and clickable** whatever the height of the window, including a window that is not full screen. The script box has a **minimum** height that still shows a few lines and no maximum that pushes the button off the panel. Nothing in the tab is reachable only by making the window bigger. |
| D-177 | the animation controls are a **floating bar** that stays with the chart | locked | The Flow animation section leaves the properties panel (D-42's section is **moved**, not copied): it becomes a **floating bar** drawn by the overlay layer, **anchored just below the chart** it controls, following it as the chart is moved or the view scrolls or zooms, and kept **inside the viewport** (pinned to the nearest edge when the chart's own edge is off screen). It carries everything the section had - play and stop, present steps, the two animation choices (D-178), the speed (D-179), the camera switch (D-170) and the loop switch - and **nothing else changes about what they do**. It is shown while a flow is selected (D-180) or playing, and not otherwise. |
| D-178 | **two animations**, one for the **nodes** and one for the **arrows**, combined | locked | The single style (D-166) becomes two choices that are **combined**: a **node animation** - none, focus, pulse - and an **arrow animation** - none, beam, marching, pipe, token. Any node animation goes with any arrow animation, and both are drawn at once over the same step order (D-43). The old single style still means what it did: a document or setting that holds a single style is read as the pair it was (focus is node focus with no arrow animation; pulse is node pulse; beam, marching, pipe and token are no node animation with that arrow animation). The choice is a **setting**, remembered between sessions like the others, and never written into the document (D-166). |
| D-179 | the **speed** of the flow is tunable | locked | The speed of the marks along the arrows - the pipe's and the marching dashes' flow, and the beam and the token - is a **setting** in scene units per second (before the chart scale of D-175), from **40 to 600**, **240** by default (D-171's fixed speed becomes the default). The control is a slider with its value shown, it changes the speed **while the animation is playing** without restarting it, and the advance stays **time-based**: the same distance in the same time at any frame rate, so doubling the speed doubles the distance covered in the same time. |
| D-180 | the animation is available **without selecting the whole chart** | locked | The floating bar appears when **any shape or arrow that belongs to a flow** is selected, and it controls the **whole connected flow** that element belongs to - the same graph the layout and the step order are built from - not just the selection. A flow that is **playing keeps playing** whatever is selected afterwards, and the overlay stays on until it is stopped; selecting something else does not hide it. The animation only ever **reads** the selection (D-166): nothing is written. |
| D-181 | the **presenter view shows the animation** | locked | The presenter window (D-64) draws the same flow animation as the editor. The editor **publishes the animation's state** - active or not, mode, node and arrow animations, speed, camera switch, the current step and the path, and the **ids of the steps** - over the existing presenter channel, in a message of its own that is sent **only when it changes** (and once when a presenter connects), and the presenter's overlay draws from it over **its own copy of the scene**, so a moved shape is followed there too. The presenter never writes an animation state back, and a presenter that is not showing a flow draws nothing and costs nothing. |
| D-182 | the board has an **edit view** and a **class view** | locked | The animation behaves differently in the two. In the **edit view** (the editor as it is) a flow animates only while the teacher plays it from the bar (D-177), exactly as in wave 7r. In the **class view** the animation is the picture, not an operation: charts that are **marked for class** (D-183) loop **by themselves**, with nothing selected and no bar. The class view is what a view-only editor already is - the editor in **view mode**, and the **presenter window** (D-64) - so there is no third mode and no new switch to forget: putting the editor in view mode is starting the class. The **published** exports (a GIF or a video) are files and a later wave; this row is the live board. |
| D-183 | **show in class**: a switch on the bar, saved with the chart | locked | The bar (D-177) gains **Show in class**. Switching it on **writes the flow's animation into the document** as ordinary element data - `customData.flow.class = { node, arrow, speed, stepMs, loop }` on **every step container of the connected flow** (D-180) - in **one undo step**, so it is saved, exported with the file, copied and pasted with the chart, and undone like anything else. The values are the bar's settings at that moment (D-178, D-179); the camera flight (D-170) is **not** part of it, because a class loop never moves the view. While the switch is on, **changing a setting on the bar writes through**, so what the teacher sees on the bar is what the class gets. Switching it off removes the entry. This is the one place an animation is written to the document, and it is a **choice the teacher makes**: playing from the bar still writes nothing (D-166). |
| D-184 | the **class loop** | locked | In the class view every marked flow **on the page being shown** plays by itself: its steps in the step order (D-43), one after another every `stepMs`, drawn with its stored node and arrow animations (D-166, D-178) at its stored speed (D-179) and its own chart scale (D-175), and **looping** unless its `loop` is off - then it plays once and **rests on its last step**. Flows are **independent**: each has its own clock, so several charts on a page animate at the same time, and a chart that is moved or resized in the editor is followed. A flow on another page is not drawn. The clock is the `AnimationController` (D-63), and **nothing runs when no marked flow is on the page**: the class view of a board with no marked flow costs nothing. |
| D-185 | **step through it**: a button on the chart | locked | In the class view each marked chart carries a small **Steps** button attached to the chart's corner and following it (placed with the library's own `sceneCoordsToViewportCoords`, D-34). Pressing it turns **that chart** from looping into **present steps** (D-167): the right and left arrows and the controls of D-167 move one step, choosing a branch works (D-172), and **Esc** or the close control returns the chart to **looping**. Only the chart that was pressed changes: the others keep looping. The arrow keys are taken only while a chart is being stepped (D-167) and are free the rest of the time. The button is not drawn in the edit view, and not in a **display-only** window (D-187). |
| D-186 | the **presenter window plays the class loop by itself** | locked | The presenter window (D-64) is always in the class view and plays every marked flow of the shown page from the **scene it already receives**: the stored settings travel in the elements, so **no new message** is needed and the loop runs even when the editor is idle. When the editor is stepping a chart (D-185), the editor's flow snapshot (D-181) reaches the presenter as before, and for **that chart** the presenter shows the mirror instead of its loop - a chart is never drawn twice - and when the stepping ends its loop resumes. The presenter writes nothing back (D-181). |
| D-187 | a **display-only** window | locked | The library gains an optional prop, `flowDisplayOnly`, for a window that shows the board and is not operated: it plays the class loop (D-184) and shows **no Steps button** (D-185) and no bar. The presenter window sets it. Without it, a view-only editor is the teacher's own view mode and has the Steps buttons. |
| D-188 | documents made before this, and charts not marked, are untouched | locked | A chart with no `customData.flow.class` never loops by itself in any view, so opening a document made before this wave changes nothing about it. The entry is read **defensively**: a missing entry, or one of the wrong type, is a flow that is **not marked**, and a partial entry takes the defaults for what it lacks. Marking a chart changes **nothing else** about its elements. |
| D-189 | the **colour of the highlight** is a setting | locked | The step highlight - the outline of **focus** and the glow of **pulse** (D-166, D-178) - takes a **colour**, `nodeColor`: a setting on the bar like the others (D-178), remembered between sessions, carried in the class entry (D-183) and in the presenter's snapshot (D-181), so the class and the projector show what the bar shows. The default is the editor's accent colour, which is what the highlight has always been. A value that is not a colour is read as the default (D-188's rule). It is the overlay's colour only: no element is changed. |
| D-190 | the colour of **what travels**, the colour of **the arrow**, and **hiding the arrow** | superseded by D-204 | Two more colours. `markColor` is the colour of what travels along an arrow - the beam, the token, the marching dashes and the pipe's highlight. `arrowColor` is the colour the **arrow itself** is drawn in while the animation plays, and it can be **hidden**: the overlay then paints the arrow in the **page's background colour**, so only what travels along it is seen. This is drawn by the overlay **over** the arrow, so the arrow in the document is **not changed** and is back the moment the animation stops (D-166). The defaults are what is drawn today: the marks in the accent colour, the pipe with its own dark and light strokes, and nothing painted over the arrow. Same carrying rules as D-189. |
| D-191 | highlight **only the text** | superseded by D-201 | A setting, `nodeTarget`: **shape** (the whole node - what it is now) or **text** (only the label inside it). With **text** the highlight is drawn on the label's own bounds and the shape is left as it is. A node with no label has no text to highlight and takes the shape. Same carrying rules as D-189. |
| D-192 | the highlight has the **node's own shape** | locked | The highlight of a step - the focus outline, the pulse glow, the dimming of steps not reached (D-167) and the branches that can be chosen (D-172) - is drawn in the shape of the node it is on, not in a rectangle: a **rectangle** with its own corner roundness, an **ellipse** as an ellipse, a **diamond** as a diamond, and a **rotated** node at its own angle. The shape is read from the container element (its type, its roundness and its angle), so a node that is changed in the editor is followed.
